1 · India and Its Location

SSC CGL Tier I · Geography · keyword revision notes · facts & ranks → extent & time → land borders → Indo-China sectors → coastal boundary & sea zones → border-guarding forces · appendix at end

Last-minute 12

  1. India = 7th largest by area (2.4% of world) · 2nd largest by population (17.7% of world's, next to China) · 28 States + 8 UTs
  2. Extreme points: N — Indira Col (Ladakh, HP-adjacent) · S (mainland) — Kanyakumari · S (overall) — Indira Point (A&N) · E — Kibithu (Arunachal) · W — Guhar Moti/Sir Creek (Gujarat)
  3. N-S extent 3,214 km > E-W extent 2,933 km — latitudes equidistant, longitudes are not, so N-S "feels" bigger
  4. IST = 82°30'E, passes through Mirzapur (UP) · 5:30 hrs ahead of GMT · MOUCA states: MP, Odisha, UP, Chhattisgarh, AP
  5. Land border total ★: 15,106.7 km — Bangladesh 4096.7 > China 3488 > Pakistan 3323 > Nepal 1751 > Myanmar 1643 > Bhutan 699 > Afghanistan 106
  6. Coastline (revised 29 Apr 2025) ★: 11,098.81 km total — Mainland 7,870.51 + Islands 3,228.30
  7. Longest coastline: UT — Andaman & Nicobar (3,083.50 km) · State — Gujarat (2,340.62 km) · Shortest — Goa (193.95 km)
  8. 3-country-border states ★: Sikkim (Nepal/China/Bhutan) · Arunachal Pradesh (Bhutan/China/Myanmar) · West Bengal (Nepal/Bhutan/Bangladesh) · UT: Ladakh (Pakistan/Afghanistan/China)
  9. Indo-China border sectors: Western (Johnson Line) · Middle · Eastern (McMahon Line) · LAC total 4,057 km
  10. Durand Line = historically Afghanistan–Pakistan border ★ — Parmar's own chapter calls it the "India-Afghanistan border," a real cross-source tension, see DOUBT below
  11. UNCLOS zones from coast: Territorial Sea 12 nm → Contiguous Zone 24 nm → EEZ 200 nm (Parmar prints "200 mm," almost certainly a typo)
  12. Border-guarding forces: ITBP→China · SSB→Nepal/Bhutan · BSF→Pakistan/Bangladesh ("India's first line of Defence")

1India — facts, ranks & extreme points

  • India was part of Gondwana land, situated in Asia
  • Area rank: 7th (2.4% of world's area) ★ — Russia, Canada, China, USA, Brazil, Australia, India, Argentina
  • Population rank: 2nd (next to China; 17.7% of world's population)
  • 28 States, 8 UTs. Largest State (area): Rajasthan · Largest UT (area): Ladakh · Smallest State: Goa · Smallest UT: Lakshadweep
  • Largest State (population): Uttar Pradesh · Largest UT (population): Delhi · Smallest State: Sikkim · Smallest UT: Lakshadweep
Extreme pointLocation
NorthernmostHimachal Pradesh / Indira Col (Ladakh)
Southernmost (mainland)Kanyakumari / Cape Comorin (Tamil Nadu)
Southernmost (overall)Indira Point / Pygmalion Point (A&N Islands)
EasternmostKibithu (Arunachal Pradesh)
WesternmostGuhar Moti / Sir Creek (Gujarat)

2Extent, Tropic of Cancer & Indian Standard Time

2.1 Latitudinal/longitudinal extent

  • Lies wholly in Northern & Eastern hemispheres, between 8°4'N–37°6'N and 68°7'E–97°25'E
  • N-S extent 3,214 km > E-W extent 2,933 km — because latitudes are equidistant everywhere but longitudes converge toward the poles, so a degree of longitude covers less ground at India's latitude than a degree of latitude does
  • Tropic of Cancer (23°30'N) divides India into two roughly equal halves, passing through 8 States
HOOKTropic-of-Cancer states — "Gujarati Raja Made Chief Justice Win The Meeting": Gujarat, Rajasthan, Madhya Pradesh, Chief=Chhattisgarh, Justice=Jharkhand, Win=West Bengal, The=Tripura, Meeting=Mizoram. Only Jaipur, Agartala, Aizawl (capitals) lie above the line.

2.2 Indian Standard Time

  • IST runs along 82°30'E, passing through Mirzapur, Uttar Pradesh ★ — 5 hrs 30 min ahead of GMT
  • Gujarat and Arunachal Pradesh differ by ~2 real hours of daylight but share one clock-time — the cost of a single time zone for a country this wide
  • Tropic of Cancer meets the IST meridian at Korea district, Baikunthpur (Chhattisgarh)
HOOKStates IST passes through — "MOUCA": Madhya Pradesh, Odisha, Uttar Pradesh, Chhattisgarh, Andhra Pradesh.
Time zones (country)France 13 (overseas territories) > Russia 11 > USA 6 — India has just 1

2.3 Indian Subcontinent — extent, area & locational advantage

  • India forms part of the continental landmass of Eurasia, on a peninsula of Southern Asia — extends from Kashmir (north) to Kanyakumari (south), Arabian Sea to the west, Bay of Bengal to the east
  • Locational advantage: sits on the world's major sea routes — maritime contact with South-West Asia & Africa (west) and South-East Asia (east); the Suez Canal route gives India trade access to North America and Europe
  • Size comparison: India's ~3.28 million sq km is nearly equal to Europe excluding Russia, and about 8× the area of Japan
Indian subcontinent (6 countries)Area
India32,87,263 sq km
Pakistan7,96,095 sq km
Bangladesh1,48,393 sq km
Nepal1,47,181 sq km
Sri Lanka65,610 sq km
Bhutan46,500 sq km
Subcontinent total44.9 lakh sq km

Subcontinent spans 3,200 km N-S and 2,933 km E-W (subcontinent-wide figures, distinct from India's own 3,214 km / 2,933 km extent in §2.1). 82°30'E, the "Standard Meridian of India," also anchors time-keeping in Nepal and Sri Lanka.

3Land border of India — country by country

HOOKBorder lengths, longest→shortest — "Ba CH PaN में MBA": Bangladesh 4096.7 · China 3488 · Pakistan 3323 · Nepal 1751 · म[M]यanmar 1643 · Bhutan 699 · Afghanistan 106 (all km). Total ★: 15,106.7 km.
CountryBorder lengthIndian States/UTs sharing itNotable line/treaty
Bangladesh4,096.7 kmWest Bengal, Assam, Meghalaya, Tripura, Mizoram (HOOK: "MAM Take Water")Radcliffe Line (1947) · India surrounds Bangladesh on 3 sides

Afghanistan106 kmLadakh (Wakhan Corridor/PoK area)Durand Line — see DOUBT below ★
DOUBTParmar's own chapter states "the border between India and Afghanistan is called the Durand Line" — but the Durand Line is historically and geographically the Afghanistan–Pakistan border (drawn 1893), which is also what the PYQ book's own tested answer confirms: "SSC CPO TIER-I, 13/12/2025 (Shift-01) — The Durand Line serves as the border between... Pakistan and Afghanistan.'" India's own tiny link to this area is only through the Wakhan Corridor/PoK, currently under Pakistani control — not a true India-Afghanistan land border in practice. For the exam, go with Pakistan–Afghanistan; Parmar's phrasing is transcribed as printed in the raw extraction but should not be taught as the primary fact.

4Indo-China border — three sectors

SectorDefining lineLength / area
Western sectorJohnson Line2,176 km · 38,000 sq km (Aksai Chin dispute)
Middle sector—554 km
Eastern sectorMcMahon Line (1914)1,327 km
Total / LAC—4,057 km total · LAC itself 520 km
  • Shaksgam Valley (Trans-Karakoram Tract, north of Siachen) — India holds this was illegally ceded to China by Pakistan under the 1963 China–Pakistan Boundary Agreement
  • Galwan River Valley (NE Ladakh, east of Aksai Chin) — also under Indian claim, currently China-occupied
  • LAC separates Indian Ladakh from Aksai Chin (a military control line, not a legally settled international border)
  • Vibrant Villages Programme — Centrally Sponsored Scheme, Union Budget 2022–23 to 2025–26, develops villages on the India-China border
TRAPLoC (Line of Control — India/Pakistan, J&K) ≠ LAC (Line of Actual Control — India/China, Ladakh/Aksai Chin) ≠ McMahon Line (India/China, Eastern sector specifically) — three different lines, three different borders, don't swap them.
HOOKHistorical border-lines map: Durand (1893, Afghanistan) · Radcliffe (1947, Pakistan & Bangladesh) · McMahon (1914, China/Tibet, Eastern sector only) · Palk Strait (Sri Lanka, a strait not a land line).

5States sharing borders with multiple countries/states

  • Maximum state-neighbours: Uttar Pradesh (8 States + Delhi UT)
  • Only-one-neighbour states: Sikkim (only West Bengal) · Meghalaya (only Assam)
  • 3-country-border states ★: Sikkim (Nepal, Bhutan, China) · Arunachal Pradesh (Bhutan, China, Myanmar) · West Bengal (Nepal, Bhutan, Bangladesh) · UT Ladakh (Pakistan, Afghanistan, China)
HOOKPYQ (SSC PHASE XIII, 30/07/2025, Shift-03) asked exactly this as a single-answer MCQ with options Sikkim/J&K(now UT)/Himachal Pradesh/Uttar Pradesh — correct answer (a) Sikkim. Note J&K is deliberately listed as "(now UT)" in the option itself, testing whether you also know J&K's 2019 reorganisation.

6.2 Sea borders & UNCLOS zones

  • India's only 2 sea-border neighbours: Sri Lanka (via Palk Strait) and Maldives
  • Gulf of Mannar — shallow sea between India and Sri Lanka, near India's southern tip
  • Kanyakumari — where Bay of Bengal, Indian Ocean and Arabian Sea meet
  • New Pamban Bridge — India's first vertical-lift sea bridge, connects Pamban (Rameswaram island) to Mandapam (mainland)
  • Definitional trivia: largest gulf in the world — Gulf of Mexico; largest bay in the world — Bay of Bengal (India's own east coast)
UNCLOS zone (1982, in force 1994)Extent
Territorial Sea12 nm (≈22 km)
Contiguous Zone24 nm
Exclusive Economic Zone (EEZ)200 nm
DOUBTParmar prints the EEZ limit as "200 mm" — almost certainly a typo for 200 nm (nautical miles), the universally-cited figure, and consistent with the correctly-printed 12 nm/24 nm just above it on the same page. Transcribed as printed in the raw extraction; do not let "200 mm" stand as a taught fact.

7Border-guarding forces (CAPF)

ForceEstablishedGuards border withMinistry

SSB1963Nepal & BhutanHome Affairs

CRPF—India's largest paramilitary force (internal security)Home Affairs

Naxalite-affected areas are called "Red Corridors." Indian Armed Forces (Army/Navy/Air Force) sit under the Ministry of Defence, distinct from all the above CAPF organisations.

A 6th question in this PYQ bucket ("rural settlements are shaped by physical features AND social structure," SSC CHSL TIER-I, 24/11/2025 Shift-02) is genuinely Human Geography content, not India-location content — cross-referenced to geo-human-geography.html rather than repeated here, since this raw-extraction file has no rural-settlement-pattern material at all.

A.2 New fact sourced only from the PYQ book (not in Parmar or Lucent)

The Integrated Check Post (ICP) at Birgunj (Nepal) – Raxaul (Bihar) is the busiest India-Nepal trade route, handling over 60% of bilateral trade in customs, immigration and other services (per the PYQ book's own explanation, SSC CGL TIER-I 14/10/2025 Shift-02). This does not appear in either source book's raw extraction — added here as a directly-tested current fact, sourced to the PYQ book's explanation text rather than to Parmar/Lucent.

A.3 Cross-book notes carried from the raw extraction

  • Lucent's border-length table (book p.158) matches Parmar's figures exactly for all 7 neighbours — a clean cross-book confirmation, not a conflict.
  • Lucent's "Indian Subcontinent" definition (book p.134) names 5 core countries + Sri Lanka "if included" = 6; a separate Lucent page (p.158) names 7 including Myanmar while still saying "these six" — almost certainly a printing/insertion error on p.158 (Myanmar doesn't belong in that specific list). Both are transcribed as printed in the raw extraction; treat book p.134's cleaner definition as primary.
  • India's coastline total (11,098.81 km) is stated identically by both Parmar and Lucent, including the exact same 13-row State/UT breakdown — a genuine cross-book confirmation.

A.4 Completeness check

Every fact in india_and_its_location_raw_extraction.md is represented above except: (1) the page's own decorative diagrams (relief-feature illustration labelling Bay/Delta/Island/Gulf/Peninsula; the IST meridian map) — their content is already captured in the running text; (2) Lucent's "Physical Divisions of the Indian Subcontinent" overview list (Pamir Knot; Great Mountain Wall of the North; Great Northern Plains; Great Peninsular Plateau; Coastal Plains; Great Indian Desert; Island Groups, book pp.134–135) — the raw extraction itself notes each division's detail is merged into its own dedicated file (himalayas_raw_extraction.md, northern_plains_and_islands_raw_extraction.md, peninsular_plateau_raw_extraction.md), so it is deliberately not repeated here; (3) Lucent's "Climatic Diversity in the Indian Subcontinent" paragraph (book p.136 — Rajasthan/Drass-Kargil temperature extremes, Mawsynram/Cherrapunji/Thar Desert rainfall figures) — this is climate content, not location/extent content, and is deferred to the future Climate of India batch rather than duplicated here.

2 · Mountainous Region and Plateaus

SSC CGL Tier I · Geography · keyword revision notes · mountain types → Himalaya formation & ranges → peaks & passes → Mt. Everest records → Peninsular Plateau & Deccan Traps · appendix at end

Last-minute 12

  1. India's 6 physiographic divisions: Himalayas, Northern Plains, Peninsular Plateau, Indian Desert, Coastal Plains, Islands
  2. Himalayas = Young Fold Mountains ★ (Indian + Eurasian plate collision, closing the Tethys Sea) · Aravallis = Old Fold Mountains (residual) ★
  3. 4 Himalayan ranges N→S: Trans-Himalayas (Karakoram/Ladakh/Zanskar) → Greater Himalayas/Himadri (avg 6000 m) → Lesser Himalayas/Himachal (avg 3500-4500 m) → Shiwaliks/Outer (avg 900-1200 m)
  4. Highest peaks: Everest 8,848 m (world, Nepal) · K2/Godwin Austen 8,611 m (India's claim, in PoK — world's 2nd) · Kanchenjunga 8,598 m (highest in India, Sikkim) · Nanda Devi 7,816 m (highest in Uttarakhand)
  5. Regional division ★: Punjab Himalaya (Indus-Sutlej, 560 km) · Kumaon (Sutlej-Kali, 320 km) · Nepal (Kali-Teesta, 800 km) · Assam (Teesta-Dihang, 720 km)
  6. Key passes: Zoji La (Srinagar↔Ladakh, Great Himalayas) ★ · Shipki La (HP/Kinnaur, Sutlej enters India) ★ · Nathu La & Jelep La (Sikkim↔Tibet) · Karakoram Pass (highest, India↔China)
  7. Everest: named after Sir George Everest · height 8,848 m (1954) → 8,850 m (GPS, 1999) → 8,848.86 m (China-Nepal joint declaration, Dec 2020) ★
  8. Everest firsts: Hillary & Tenzing (1953, first ascent) · Junko Tabei (1975, first woman) · Bachhendri Pal (1984, first Indian woman) · Kami Rita Sherpa (23 times, most ascents)
  9. Peninsular Plateau = India's oldest & largest physiographic division, part of Gondwana land, elevation 600-900 m; 2 parts — Central Highlands (north of Narmada) + Deccan Plateau (south of Narmada) ★
  10. Deccan Traps = basaltic lava from the Réunion hotspot as the Indian Plate drifted north → source of black/Regur soil ★
  11. Chotanagpur Plateau = "Ruhr of India" (mineral wealth) · highest peak Parasnath Hill (1,365 m, Jharkhand) · Meghalaya Plateau/Garo-Khasi-Jaintia Hills = its NE extension, split off by the Malda fault/gap ★
  12. Plateau region ≈ 27% of India's total land area ★

1Mountain types & formation of the Himalayas

1.1 Types of mountains

TypeFormed by / examples
Fold — YoungTectonic collision, recent. Himalayas, Andes (world's longest range), Alps, Rockies, Atlas, Caucasus
Fold — OldAncient folding, eroded/residual. Aravalli, Ural (Asia/Europe divide), Appalachians
BlockCrust broken & displaced — Horst (raised) / Graben=Rift Valley (lowered). Vindhya & Satpura, Vosges (France), Harz (Germany), Great African Rift Valley (world's longest)
VolcanicMagma eruption/lava build-up. Stromboli ("Lighthouse of Mediterranean"), Cotopaxi (Ecuador), Fujiyama (Japan), Barren & Narcondam (A&N Islands), Mt. Kilimanjaro (Africa), Ojos del Salado (world's highest active volcano, Chile-Argentina)
DOUBTParmar prints the volcano's name as "Ojas del Salado" — the standard spelling is Ojos del Salado; almost certainly a printing slip. Transcribed as printed in the raw extraction.

1.2 Formation of the Himalayas

  • Formed by collision of the Indian(-Australian) and Eurasian tectonic plates ★ — closed the ancient Tethys Sea, folding & uplifting its seabed sediments
  • Geosyncline: obsolete term for the long trough where these sediments first accumulated
  • Formed ~4-5 crore years ago ★ — one of the world's youngest fold-mountain ranges, mainly sedimentary rock
TRAPHimalayas = young fold mountains (tectonic collision, still rising) ≠ Aravallis = old fold mountains (residual, heavily eroded, among the world's oldest mountain systems) — a PYQ tested exactly this contrast as an Assertion-Reason question.

2The four Himalayan ranges (Trans- → Greater → Lesser → Shiwalik)

RangeAvg heightWidthNotes
Trans-Himalayas3,000 m—North of Greater Himalayas; high, arid plateaus. Contains Karakoram (K2/Godwin-Austen 8,611 m, also Mt. Rakaposhi & Mt. Gasherbrum), Ladakh Range (Shyok flows between Karakoram & Ladakh), Zanskar Range (Kamet peak; Indus flows between Ladakh & Zanskar). Kailash Range (China) also part of it.
Greater Himalayas / Himadri6,000 m120–190 kmAxial range 2,500 km; core of granitic rock; asymmetrical. Westernmost: Nanga Parbat (8,126 m); Easternmost: Namcha Barwa (syntaxial bend). Contains Everest, Kanchenjunga, Lhotse, Makalu, Dhaulagiri ("White Mountain"), Annapurna, Nanda Devi, Kamet
Lesser Himalayas / Himachal3,500–4,500 m (Parmar: avg 4,000 m)60–80 kmState-wise names: J&K = Pir Panjal; HP = Dhauladhar; Uttarakhand = Nagtibba; Nepal = Mahabharat Range. Tourist hubs Shimla/Mussoorie/Nainital sit here (per Lucent)
Shiwaliks / Outer900–1,200 m10–50 kmBroader in the west; disappears eastward, replaced by Duars (tea-growing lowlands). Duns = longitudinal valleys between Lesser Himalayas & Shiwaliks — largest is Dehradun. Nepal's Shiwalik stretch = Churia Range
HOOKN→S order — "Trans → Greater → Lesser → Shiwalik": Tiger Goes Looking South. Heights fall as you go south: 3,000/6,000 → 3,500-4,500 → 900-1,200 m.
TRAPLucent names only 3 ranges (Greater/Lesser/Outer) as "the Himalayas" proper — Parmar's own 4-range version additionally names Trans-Himalayas as a distinct northern range. Not a conflict — Lucent is just describing the narrower "Himalayas proper," while Trans-Himalayas is geologically adjacent but separately classified by some sources.
HOOKKashmir Valley sits between Greater & Lesser Himalayas; important passes here: Zoji La (Greater Himalayas) ★, Banihal (Pir Panjal), Photu La & Khardung La (Zanskar). Karewa formations (glacial+lacustrine) here grow saffron (Zafran). Freshwater lakes: Dal, Wular. Saltwater: Pangong Tso, Tso Moriri.
HOOKPamir Knot ("Roof of the World") = junction of the Tian Shan, Karakoram, Kunlun & Hindu Kush ranges; the Tibetan Plateau is also separately called "Roof of the World." Mount Kunlun ("Ancestor of Mountains") is not part of the Himalayas — it formed from the same Indo-Australian/Eurasian plate collision but is classified separately.

2.1 Siachen & other Karakoram glaciers

  • Siachen Glacier: 2nd-longest non-polar glacier · world's highest battlefield (5,700 m+) · India holds it since Operation Meghdoot (1984)
  • Also in the Karakoram: Baltoro (Gilgit-Baltistan), Hispar, Biafo
  • World's longest non-polar glacier: Fedchenko Glacier (Tajikistan). World's longest glacier overall: Lambert Glacier (Antarctica)

3Highest peaks & regional division

3.0 Highest peaks of the Greater Himalayas (Parmar's own list)

  • 1. Everest 8,848 m — Sagarmatha (Nepal)/Chomolungma (Tibet) · 2. Kanchenjunga 8,598 m (highest in India) · 3. Lhotse 8,516 m (Tibet/Nepal) · 4. Makalu 8,485 m
  • 5. Dhaulagiri 8,167 m ("White Mountain") · 6. Annapurna 8,091 m (4-6 grouped as "Nepal" peaks) · 7. Nanda Devi 7,816 m (highest in Uttarakhand) · 8. Kamet 7,756 m (Uttarakhand)
  • Other named Greater Himalayan peaks (Uttarakhand): Kedarnath, Badrinath, Bandarpunch; Gaurishankar lies in Nepal
TRAPThis 8-peak list (§3.0, Parmar's "Highest Peaks of Greater Himalayas") ≠ the separate Lucent elevation table below (§3.1, "Heights of Major Mountain Peaks in India") — the two overlap on Everest/Kanchenjunga/Nanda Devi/Kamet but the rest differ (Lucent's list is India-specific and includes several PoK-situated peaks like K2/Nanga Parbat that Parmar's world-peaks list omits).

3.2 Regional division of the Himalayas ★

DivisionLengthBetween rivers
Punjab Himalaya560 kmIndus & Sutlej
Kumaon Himalaya320 kmSutlej & Kali
Nepal Himalaya800 kmKali & Teesta
Assam Himalaya720 kmTeesta & Dihang
HOOK"Punjab Kumaon Nepal Assam" west→east, lengths 560/320/800/720 — Nepal segment is the longest.

4North-East ranges & important passes

4.1 Arunachal & Purvanchal (Eastern) Hills

  • Arunachal Himalayas: eastern border of Eastern Himalayas; peaks Kangto (highest of AP) & Namcha Barwa; rivers Kameng/Subansiri/Dibang; tribes Monpa/Dafla/Miri/Abor/Mishmi/Nyishi/Naga (practice Jhumming)
  • Purvanchal Hills: N-S alignment, Arunachal → Mizoram. Key peaks: Naga Hills (Saramati, Nagaland), Manipur Hills (Mt. Iso/Esii/Tempo), Mizo/Lushai Hills (Mt. Phawngpui/"Blue Mountain")
  • Manipur/Mizoram = "Molasses Basin," drained by the Barak river. Manipur's Loktak Lake = world's only floating lake; Keibul Lamjao NP = world's only floating National Park
  • Barail Range (Nagaland/Manipur–Assam/Meghalaya) = watershed between Brahmaputra & Barak. Garo/Khasi/Jaintia/North Cachar Hills are not Himalayan — they belong to the Peninsular Plateau (see §6)

4.1b Regional Himalaya belts — states, tribes & orientation

  • Himachal & Uttarakhand Himalayas: span J&K/HP/Uttarakhand, drained by the Indus & Ganga systems. Bhotia tribe lives in the Greater Himalayan valleys — nomadic, moves to 'Bugyals' (summer grasslands) in summer
  • Darjeeling & Sikkim Himalayas: home to Kanchenjunga (India's highest peak); Lepcha and Bhutia tribes live at higher elevations; Duar formations seen here too (a PYQ tested the Munnar (Western Ghats) vs Ladakh-Nubra (Trans-Himalayas) vs Darjeeling-Siliguri (Eastern Himalayas/Terai) distinction ★ — see appendix)
  • Orientation of Himalayas by state: J&K = NW-SE · Sikkim = E-W · Arunachal = SW-NE · Eastern Himalayas overall = N-S

4.2 Important mountain passes by state

J&K/LadakhKarakoram (highest, India↔China) ★ · Pir Panjal & Banihal (Jammu↔Srinagar) · Zoji La (Srinagar↔Leh-Ladakh) ★ · Khardung La (Leh↔Nubra) · Mig La — world's highest motorable road, built under Project Himank · Pensi La (Leh↔Kashmir, via Zanskar)
Himachal PradeshShipki La (Kinnaur↔Tibet; Sutlej enters India here) ★ · Rohtang (Kullu↔Lahaul-Spiti; Atal Tunnel beneath it) · Bara-Lacha La
UttarakhandMana & Niti (↔Tibet) · Lipu Lekh (Uttarakhand-Tibet-Nepal tri-junction)
SikkimNathu La & Jelep La (↔Tibet)
Arunachal PradeshBum La (China↔Tawang) · Bomdila/Diphu/Pangsau (↔Myanmar) · Sela Pass (Tawang↔West Kameng, sacred in Tibetan Buddhism)
DOUBTParmar prints "Bom Di La Pass connects Arunachal Pradesh with Lhase (Tibet)" — almost certainly meant to read Lhasa. Transcribed as printed.

5Mount Everest — facts & records

  • Named after Sir George Everest, Surveyor General of India (1830 to 1843 A.D.); earlier known as Peak-15
  • Height: 8,848 m (measured 1954) → 8,850 m (GPS satellite, Geographic Society, 5 May 1999) → 8,848.86 m / 29,031.69 ft ★ (China-Nepal joint declaration, Dec 2020)
  • Called Sagarmatha ("Goddess of the Sky") in Nepal, Komolangma/Chomolungma ("Goddess of Ice") in Tibet
  • Location: 85°55'40"E, 27°59'16"N
First ascentEdmund Hillary & Tenzing Norgay, 1953
First without oxygenMessner & Habeler, 8 May 1978
First to scale twiceNowang Gombu (1963 & 1965) — Tenzing Norgay's nephew
First AmericanJim Whittaker, 1 May 1963
First woman (world)Junko Tabei (Japan), 1975
First Indian womanBachhendri Pal, 23 May 1984
First woman w/o oxygenLydia Bradey (NZ), 22 May 1988
First Indian w/o oxygenPhu Dorjee, 5 May 1984
Most ascents (overall)Kami Rita Sherpa — 23 times (till 21 May 2019)
Most ascents (female)Lhakpa Sherpa — 9 times (2000–2018)
Youngest male (world)Jordan Romero (USA), age 13, 22 May 2010
Youngest female (world)Malavath Poorna, age 13, 25 May 2014
Youngest Indian maleRaghav Juneja (Moradabad), age 15, 21 May 2013
First disabled climberTom Whittaker (artificial leg), 1998
First with haemophiliaChris Bombardier (Denver, USA), 22 May 2017

"There is no age bar to scale Mt. Everest from the Tibetan side" (as printed). Arjun Vajpai (NOIDA) scaled it at 16, 13 May 2010.

6Peninsular Plateau — overview, Central Highlands & Deccan

HOOKPeninsular Plateau = India's oldest & largest physiographic unit — part of Gondwana land, old crystalline/igneous/metamorphic rock, elevation 600-900 m (Lucent: 300-900 m), 2 parts split by the Narmada: Central Highlands (north) + Deccan Plateau (south) ★.

6.1 Central Highlands

  • North of Narmada, bordered by Aravallis (NW) & Vindhyas (S); elevation 700-1,000 m; wider in west, narrower east — extends into Chotanagpur Plateau (drained by Damodar)
  • Malwa Plateau: Deccan Traps extension (Rajasthan-MP), black soil; rivers Mahi/Chambal/Betwa/Sindh/Ken
  • Bundelkhand (UP-MP, 300-600 m) · Baghelkhand (UP-MP)
  • Chotanagpur Plateau (Jharkhand/Chhattisgarh/Odisha/WB): "Ruhr of India" (mineral wealth); Netarhat = "Queen of Chotanagpur"; comprises Ranchi/Hazaribagh/Koderma plateaus; highest peak Parasnath Hill (1,365 m) ★; Jadugoda (Jharkhand) = Uranium mines
  • Aravallis: SW-NE old fold mountains, Delhi→Gujarat via Haryana/Rajasthan, elevation ~800 m; highest peak Guru Shikhar (1,722 m, Mt. Abu). Rashtrapati Bhavan sits on Raisina Hill; Dilwara Jain Temple on Mt. Abu

6.2 Satpura, Vindhya & the Deccan Plateau

  • Satpura Range: block mountain, N of Deccan Plateau, parallel to Vindhyas (MP/Maharashtra/Chhattisgarh/Gujarat). 4 hills: Mahadeo (highest pt. Dhupgarh, 1,350 m) · Maikal (highest = Amarkantak plateau, source of Narmada & Son; rich in Bauxite) · Asirgarh Hills ("Key of Deccan" fort) · Rajpipla Hills. Pachmarhi = "Queen of Satpura"
  • Vindhya Range: block mountain (Gujarat/Rajasthan/MP); highest point Sadbhawna Shikhar/Kalumar Peak (752 m); Panna (MP) = diamond mines; Kalinjar Fort (Bundelkhand, UP) = National Geo-Heritage Site
  • Deccan Plateau: triangular, south of Narmada — Kerala/TN/AP/Telangana/Karnataka/Maharashtra; bounded by Western & Eastern Ghats; higher in west, slopes gently east (rivers flow east) ★. NE extension = Meghalaya/Karbi-Anglong Plateau/North Cachar Hills, split from Chotanagpur by the Malda fault/gap ★
  • Formation of Deccan Traps ★: after breaking from Gondwana, the Indian Plate drifted north toward Eurasia, passing over the Reunion Islands hotspot (near Mauritius/Seychelles) → massive basaltic lava eruptions → black/Regur soil
TRAPGaro, Khasi, Jaintia & North Cachar Hills (Meghalaya/Karbi-Anglong Plateau) look Himalayan on a map but are classified as a Peninsular-Plateau extension, split off from Chotanagpur/Rajmahal Hills by the Malda fault — a PYQ tested this exact Assertion-Reason pairing.

7Major Plateaus & land-area share

  • Lucent's Major Plateaus list: Marwar Upland, Central Highland, Bundelkhand, Malwa, Baghelkhand, Chotanagpur (+ Hazaribagh/Ranchi/Rajmahal Hills), Meghalaya, Deccan, Maharashtra, Karnataka, Telangana Plateaus, Chhattisgarh Plain (Chhattisgarh is conventionally a plain/basin — its inclusion here is the source's own category looseness, not a transcription error)
  • Plateau region covers roughly 27% of India's total land area ★

A.2 Cross-book notes carried from the raw extraction

  • Lucent's 3-range Himalaya division (Greater/Lesser/Outer) is not a conflict with Parmar's 4-range version — Lucent simply doesn't separately classify Trans-Himalayas as part of "the Himalayas proper."
  • Lucent's Major Passes table (17 passes) and Peak-heights table cross-confirm Parmar's own passes/peaks content with no attribution conflicts; the Heights of Major Mountain Peaks table (with PoK asterisks) is genuinely new detail not in Parmar's own prose.
  • Parmar spells it "Chotanagpur," Lucent "Chhottanagpur" — both transcribed as printed, not unified.
  • Mt. Saramati appears twice in Parmar's own Himalayas + Peninsular-Plateau-adjacent chapters, consistently attributed to Nagaland both times — a repetition, not a conflict.

A.3 Doubts carried from the raw extraction (unresolved spelling/attribution points)

  • "Ojas del Salado" (almost certainly Ojos del Salado) and "Bom Di La Pass...with Lhase" (almost certainly Lhasa) — both printing slips, transcribed as printed, not corrected.
  • Pamir's map-annotation epithet "Roof of Himalayas" doesn't match the book's own two separate "Roof of the World" statements (for the Pamir Knot and for the Tibetan Plateau) — flagged, not unified.
  • The Haemophilia-climber's surname is printed "Bombadiar" and his city "Denvar" (almost certainly Bombardier/Denver) — transcribed as printed above using the corrected common spellings for readability, per this build's practice of using clearly-correct forms in the keyword notes while flagging the source anomaly here (see raw extraction for the exact-as-printed transcription).

Jhopuno) — low-value name-only trivia, deliberately not enumerated exhaustively; (5) one further Chris Bombardier detail ("scaled all highest peaks of every continent except Vinson") — a secondary fact about an already-covered record-holder, omitted for space.

3 · Eastern And Western Ghats

SSC CGL Tier I · Geography · keyword revision notes · Ghats comparison table → passes → state-wise hills → Nilgiri & Anamudi → biodiversity/UNESCO angle · appendix at end

Last-minute 12

  1. Western & Eastern Ghats mark the west/east edges of the Deccan Plateau; they meet at the Nilgiri Hills (Tamil Nadu) ★
  2. Western Ghats = continuous block mountains, crossed only through passes · Eastern Ghats = discontinuous, dissected by rivers ★
  3. Average height: Western 1,200 m (higher) vs Eastern 600 m (lower, narrower)
  4. Extent: Western — Tapi to south of Nilgiris · Eastern — Mahanadi Valley to the Nilgiris
  5. Highest peaks ★: Western — Anamudi/Anaimudi (2,695 m, Annamalai Hills, Kerala — highest peak of the whole Peninsula) · Eastern — Jindhagada (1,690 m) & Mahendragiri (1,501 m, Odisha)
  6. In Maharashtra the Western Ghats are called Sahyadri Hills, rising in height North→South
  7. Western Ghats passes: Thal Ghat (Mumbai↔Nasik) · Bhor Ghat (Mumbai↔Pune) · Pal Ghat/Palakkad Gap (Coimbatore, TN ↔ Palakkad, Kerala)
  8. Nilgiri Hills ★: tri-junction of Kerala, Tamil Nadu, Karnataka; highest peak Dodabetta (2,637 m, 2nd highest of South India/Western Ghats); hill station Ooty ("Queen of Hills," Nilgiri Biosphere Reserve)
  9. Rain-shadow effect ★: Western Ghats intercept moisture-laden Arabian-Sea winds → orographic rain on the windward (west) slope, dry leeward (east) side — e.g. Pune/interior Deccan
  10. Western Ghats = UNESCO World Heritage Site & biodiversity hotspot ★ — influences the Indian monsoon; threatened by invasive species (Lantana camara, Prosopis juliflora, Eucalyptus) more than by habitat loss alone
  11. Bhima Fault (Karnataka/Maharashtra, in the plateau) = zone of recurrent seismic activity
  12. Famous hills: Shatrunjaya & Dhinodhar (Gujarat) · Kalsubai Peak (Maharashtra, highest peak of the state) · Mt. Saramati (Nagaland) · Anantagiri Hills (AP, Borra Caves)

1Western vs Eastern Ghats — comparison

FeatureWestern GhatsEastern Ghats
ContinuityContinuous — crossed only through passesDiscontinuous, irregular, dissected by rivers
Average height1,200 m (higher)600 m (lower, narrower)
ExtentTapi (river) to south of Nilgiri HillsMahanadi Valley to the Nilgiri Hills (south)

HOOKBoth Ghats mark the edges of the Deccan Plateau and meet at the Nilgiri Hills (Tamil Nadu) — Western is taller/unbroken, Eastern is shorter/broken-up, mnemonic "Western = Wall (continuous), Eastern = Eroded (gaps)."
HOOKIn Maharashtra, Western Ghats = Sahyadri Hills, rising in height from north to south.

2Passes & state-wise hills of the Western Ghats

2.1 Important passes

Thal GhatMumbai ↔ Nasik
Bhor GhatMumbai ↔ Pune
Pal Ghat / Palakkad GapCoimbatore (TN) ↔ Palakkad (Kerala); also the Nilgiri–Annamalai link

2.2 Hills & ranges by state

  • Gujarat: Barda, Girnar, Mandav, Garda hills
  • Maharashtra: Ajanta, Satmala, Balaghat, Harishchandra, Gavilgarh range, Mahabaleshwar, Kalsubai peak
  • Karnataka: Badami, Bagalkot, Chitradurga, Shimoga, Baba Budan hills
  • Tamil Nadu: Nilgiri, Palani, Varusanadu hills
  • Kerala: Nilgiri, Annamalai (Anamudi peak — highest of Western Ghats), Cardamom, Palakkad hills (Palakkad Gap)

3Nilgiri Hills & the meeting of the two Ghats

  • Nilgiri = tri-junction of Kerala, Tamil Nadu and Karnataka ★ — also the point where the Eastern and Western Ghats meet
  • Highest peak: Dodabetta (2,637 m) — 2nd-highest peak of South India & the Western Ghats (after Anamudi)
  • Ooty (Udhagamandalam), the "Queen of Hills," is the famous hill station here, inside the Nilgiri Biosphere Reserve — distinct from Shimla/Manali/Darjeeling, which sit in the Himalayas, not the Nilgiris
TRAPNilgiri hill stations vs Himalayan hill stations: Ooty (Nilgiris, Western Ghats) ≠ Shimla, Manali, Darjeeling (all Himalayan) — a PYQ tested exactly this distinction.

4State-wise hills of the Eastern Ghats & the Bhima Fault

  • Tamil Nadu: Pachaimalai, Javadi hills
  • Andhra Pradesh: Nallamala, Velikonda, Palkonda ranges, Seshachalam hills (Tirupati temple is here)
  • Odisha: Bamra, Kanaka, Khondamal hills. Garhjat Hills (Odisha) belong to the Central Highlands but mark the starting point of the Eastern Ghats.
  • Bhima Fault (mainly Karnataka & Maharashtra, within the peninsular plateau) — known for recurrent seismic activity
HOOKSome Famous Hills — Dhinodhar & Shatrunjaya (Gujarat) · Kalsubai Peak (Maharashtra, its highest point) · Mt. Saramati (Nagaland) · Anantagiri Hills (AP — Borra Caves).

5Rain shadow, monsoon & biodiversity — the tested angle

  • Rain-shadow effect ★: Western Ghats intercept moisture-laden winds from the Arabian Sea → heavy orographic rain on the windward (western) slope; by the time winds cross to the leeward (eastern) side they're dry — e.g. Pune & the interior Deccan Plateau sit in this rain shadow
  • Western Ghats act as the principal water divide of Peninsular India ★ — rivers like the Godavari, Krishna and Kaveri rise here and flow east into the Bay of Bengal
  • Western Ghats = UNESCO World Heritage Site ★ and one of the world's major biodiversity hotspots — high endemism, and it shapes the Indian monsoon's movement
  • Biggest current threat to endemic species (beyond general habitat loss): invasive alien species ★ — e.g. Lantana camara, Prosopis juliflora, Eucalyptus, which displace native vegetation
  • New/endemic-species trivia: Allographa effusoredica, a newly-discovered lichen species, was found in the Western Ghats ★

  • Lucent's description of the Deccan-trap/Ghats geography (book p.135) matches Parmar's own PART A treatment closely, including the west-flowing Narmada/Tapti (no deltas, enter the Arabian Sea) vs east-flowing Godavari/Mahanadi/Krishna/Cauvery (delta-building, enter the Bay of Bengal) detail — already covered in Batch 2's Deccan Plateau section, not repeated here.

4 · Plains, Ports and Islands

SSC CGL Tier I · Geography · keyword revision notes · Northern Plains formation & sub-types → Coastal Plains → Major Ports → Thar Desert → Lakshadweep & Andaman-Nicobar → other islands · appendix at end

Last-minute 12

  1. Northern Plains = alluvium from the Indus-Ganga-Brahmaputra system; length 3,200 km (2,400 km in India), width 150-300 km, alluvial depth 1,000-2,000 m ★
  2. N→S cross-section: Himalaya → Bhabar (porous, no farming) → Terai (marshy, fertile — Corbett/Dudhwa/Valmiki/Rajaji NPs) → Khadar (new alluvium, most fertile) / Bhangar (old, kankar-rich, less fertile)
  3. Northern Plains = India's most densely populated & most agriculturally productive region ★ — fertile alluvial soil (Khadar/Bhangar), flat terrain, ample water → both Rabi (wheat) & Kharif (rice) grown here
  4. Western Coastal Plain = narrow, "submerged" (dumbbell-shaped), no deltas (rivers rush to sea) — narrow because the Western Ghats sit close behind it ★ — Konkan (Gujarat→Goa) + Malabar (Goa→Kanyakumari)
  5. Eastern Coastal Plain = broader, "emerging," big river deltas (Ganga/Mahanadi/Godavari/Krishna/Kaveri) — Northern Circars + Coromandel Coast (Krishna delta, AP → Kanyakumari, TN) ★
  6. 13 Major Ports of India — only Kamarajar/Ennore (Tamil Nadu) is corporate/privately managed; the other 12 are Central-Govt-owned ★
  7. Port superlatives: Mumbai = largest natural harbour · JNPT/Nhava Sheva = largest container port · Kolkata = only riverine port · Vishakhapatnam = deepest landlocked port · Tuticorin = southernmost port · Cochin = "Queen of the Arabian Sea" · Vizhinjam (Kerala) = India's first transhipment port
  8. Thar/Great Indian Desert: NW of the Aravallis; ~150 mm rain/yr; "Marusthali" = "Land of Dead"; rivers ephemeral; in the rain-shadow of the SW monsoon (Aravallis run parallel to the wind, so block nothing) ★
  9. India's 2 island groups: Lakshadweep (Arabian Sea, coral, 36 islands) vs Andaman & Nicobar (Bay of Bengal, ~572 islands, submerged Arakan Yoma extension)
  10. Lakshadweep: largest = Andrott · 2nd = Minicoy · smallest = Bitra · Aminidivi group (above 11° Channel) vs Cannanore group (below it)
  11. A&N: highest peak Saddle Peak (North Andaman) · only active volcano = Barren Island; dormant = Narcondam · Ten Degree Channel splits Andaman from Nicobar (10°N) ★
  12. Capital renamed Port Blair → Sri Vijaya Puram; other renames: Ross→Netaji Subhas Chandra Bose Island, Neil→Shaheed Dweep, Havelock→Swaraj Dweep. Indira Point (formerly Pygmalion Point, Great Nicobar) = India's southernmost point

NP National Park · A&N Andaman & Nicobar · UT Union Territory

1Northern Plains — formation & classification

  • Formed by alluvial deposition from the Indus, Ganga and Brahmaputra systems ★ — flat, fertile, ideal for farming
  • Length 3,200 km (2,400 km within India) · avg width 150-300 km · alluvium depth 1,000-2,000 m
Belt (N→S)Character
BhabarNarrow (8-16 km), south of Shiwaliks; streams deposit stones/pebbles then vanish underground — too porous for farming
TeraiSouth of Bhabar (10-20 km); streams re-emerge, marshy, lush & fertile. Key NPs: Jim Corbett (Uttarakhand), Dudhwa (UP), Valmiki (Bihar), Rajaji (Uttarakhand), Chitwan (Nepal)
KhadarNew alluvium, renewed annually by floods — most fertile, intensive farming
BhangarOld alluvium, elevated, kankar/calcareous-rich — less fertile than Khadar
HOOKN→S: "Himalaya Babbles Terrific Khabar" (Himalaya → Bhabar → Terai → Khadar/Bhangar) — porosity falls, fertility rises, as you move away from the mountains.
TRAPA PYQ tested this directly: the Northern Plain is not "rocky and arid" despite sitting next to the Himalayas — it's a level alluvial plain, and it is not sparsely populated/infertile either — it's India's most densely populated, most fertile region. Both the "rocky/arid" and "sparse/infertile" framings are wrong.

1.1 The three sub-divisions (Lucent)

  • Punjab-Haryana Plains: part of the Indus basin, separated from the Ganga plains by a low watershed
  • Ganga Plains: largest lowland, drained by the Ganga + tributaries — Yamuna (most important), Ghaghara, Gandak, Kosi (from Himalayas); Son (flows north) & Damodar (from the plateau); Chambal & Betwa (peninsular, feed the Yamuna). Gentle slope, flood-prone; Ganga+Brahmaputra together form a large delta downstream
  • Brahmaputra Valley: the third sub-division (Assam)

2Coastal Plains — West vs East

FeatureWestern Coastal PlainEastern Coastal Plain
Shape/characterNarrow, dumbbell-shaped (narrow middle, wider ends); submerged coast (land sunk below sea level)Broader; emerging coastline, large river deltas
Why narrow/broadWestern Ghats sit close behind → rivers rush to sea, no deltas ★Gentle slope → Ganga/Mahanadi/Godavari/Krishna/Kaveri build deltas
Sub-regionsKonkan (Gujarat→Goa) · Malabar (Goa→Kanyakumari)Northern Circars (Odisha→) · Coromandel Coast (Krishna delta, AP → Kanyakumari, TN) ★
Water bodiesBackwaters/Kayals (e.g. Punnamada Kayal, Kerala — Nehru Trophy Boat Race)Lagoons — Chilika (Odisha) & Pulicat lakes
Best known forPort development (Kandla→Cochin, north to south)Rice cultivation; less suited to ports
HOOKKonkan/Malabar (west) vs Northern Circars/Coromandel (east) — both coasts extend to Kanyakumari at their southern end, meeting at India's tip.

3Major Ports of India

PortStateSpecial feature

  • 13 Major Ports total: 12 public (Central Govt-owned) + 1 private (Kamarajar/Ennore) ★
  • Vizhinjam (Kerala) = India's first transhipment port; Vadhavan Port (Maharashtra) shown on the map but not yet in the 13-port table — likely a newer/upcoming addition

4The Great Indian (Thar) Desert

  • Lies NW of the Aravalli Range (Parmar) / west of it (Lucent) — extends across Rajasthan and into Sindh, Pakistan
  • ~150 mm rainfall/year; hot summers, cold dry winters; rivers mostly ephemeral (rain-season only)
  • "Marusthali" = "Land of Dead" (as printed; more common English rendering is "Land of the the Dead")
  • Believed submerged under the sea during the Mesozoic era; Lake Sambhar (salt lake) is here
  • Rain-shadow reasoning ★: the Aravallis run roughly parallel to the SW monsoon winds rather than across them, so they don't block/lift the moisture the way the Western Ghats do — this is why the desert stays dry, not (as Lucent's own phrasing oddly puts it) a "rain shadow of the Bay of Bengal current"
DOUBTLucent describes the Thar as being "in the rain shadow area of the Bay of Bengal current" — rain-shadow effects are conventionally attributed to a mountain range blocking monsoon winds (e.g. Aravallis vs the SW monsoon), not to an ocean "current." Transcribed as printed; treat the mountain-blocking explanation as the standard/exam-safe one.

5Islands — classification & India's two groups

5.1 Classification by origin (Lucent)

TectonicLand subsidence/uplift, rift valleys. E.g. West Indies
VolcanicVolcanic eruption material emerges above water. E.g. Hawaiian Islands
ErosionalSoft rock erodes away, hard rock remains. E.g. Greenland
DepositionalRiver/glacier/wave sediment deposition
CoralCoral-polyp accumulation on tropical sub-marine platforms. E.g. Lakshadweep, Maldives

A group of islands = Archipelago.

5.2 India's two island groups — quick contrast

SeaLakshadweep — Arabian Sea  |  A&N — Bay of Bengal
OriginLakshadweep — coral  |  A&N — volcanic/submerged Arakan Yoma (Myanmar) extension
Count36  |  ~572 (cross-book figure, not independently verified — counts vary by source/definition)
Area30-32 sq km  |  8,249 sq km
Lat/Long extent8°N-12°N, 71°E-74°E  |  6°N-14°N, 92°E-94°E

6Lakshadweep in detail

  • Largest island: Andrott · 2nd largest: Minicoy · smallest: Bitra
  • Above the 11° Channel: Aminidivi group (Bitra, Andrott, Amini, Kadmat) · below it: Cannanore group (Kavaratti [the UT capital], Suheli, Agatti, Kalpeni)
  • Channels: 8° Channel (Lakshadweep/Minicoy ↔ Maldives) · 9° Channel (Lakshadweep ↔ Minicoy) · 11° Channel (Aminidivi ↔ Cannanore)
  • Located ~300 km west of the Kerala coast (per Lucent)

7Andaman & Nicobar in detail

7.1 Peaks, channels & volcanoes

Saddle PeakNorth Andaman — highest (732/738 m)
Mount ThullierGreat Nicobar — 2nd highest (642 m)
Mt. DiavoloMiddle Andaman (295/515 m — figures differ across sources)
Mt. KoyobSouth Andaman (495/460 m)
  • Barren Island = India's only active volcano (NE of South Andaman) · Narcondam = dormant volcanic island, further north
  • Ten Degree Channel ★ separates Andaman from Nicobar (10°N latitude) · Duncan Passage: South Andaman ↔ Little Andaman · Coco Channel: A&N ↔ Myanmar
  • Largest island: Middle Andaman

7.2 Tribes, renamed islands & National Parks

  • Indigenous tribes — Andaman: North Andamanese, Jarawa, Onge, Sentinelese. Nicobar: Shompen, Nicobarese
  • Renamed: Ross Island → Netaji Subhas Chandra Bose Island · Neil Island → Shaheed Dweep · Havelock Island → Swaraj Dweep. Capital Port Blair → Sri Vijaya Puram
  • 21 unnamed islands renamed after Param Vir Chakra awardees by PM Modi, 23 Jan 2023
  • Key NPs: Saddle Peak (N. Andaman) · Mount Harriet & Mahatma Gandhi NP (S. Andaman) · Rani Jhansi Marine NP (Ritchie's Archipelago) · North/Middle/South Button Island NPs · Campbell Bay & Galathea Bay NP (Great Nicobar)
  • Cellular Jail — Port Blair (South Andaman)
HOOKIndira Point (formerly Pygmalion Point), Great Nicobar Island = India's southernmost point overall (Kanyakumari is the southernmost mainland point). Great/Six Degree Channel separates Great Nicobar from Indonesia's Sumatra.

8Other named islands (west coast, east coast, riverine)

8.1 West coast (Arabian Sea)

  • Diu (ex-Portuguese, now Dadra & Nagar Haveli/Daman & Diu UT) · Piram Bet & Bet Dwarka (Gulf of Khambhat area, Gujarat) · Alia Bet (Narmada delta, Gujarat)
  • Elephanta Island (Mumbai Harbour — Elephanta Caves, UNESCO WHS) · Salsette Island (Mumbai + Thane sit on it) · Vengurla Rocks (Maharashtra) · St. Mary's Islands (Malpe, Karnataka — columnar basalt)

8.2 East coast (Bay of Bengal) & riverine

  • Sagar Island (Gangasagar, in the Ganges Delta, WB — Hindu pilgrimage site) · New Moore/Purbasha Island (disputed India-Bangladesh, now largely submerged) · Abdul Kalam/Wheeler Island (Odisha — missile testing) · Sriharikota (AP — home to the Satish Dhawan Space Centre (SDSC), primary launch site of the Indian Space Research Organisation (ISRO)) ★ · Parikud Island (Odisha)
  • Riverine: Majuli (Brahmaputra, Assam — once world's largest river island) · Srirangam (Kaveri, Tamil Nadu — Ranganatha Swamy Temple)
  • Pamban/Rameswaram Island (Ramanatha Swamy Temple, Pamban Bridge) · Gulf of Mannar (India-Sri Lanka) · Chorao & Divar Islands (Goa; Chorao = Dr. Salim Ali Bird Sanctuary) · Katchatheevu Island (Palk Strait, disputed with Sri Lanka) · Sir Creek (disputed with Pakistan)
  • Palk Strait's SW portion = Palk Bay

A.2 Cross-book notes carried from the raw extraction

  • Lucent's islands-by-origin classification (Tectonic/Volcanic/Erosional/Depositional/Coral) is genuinely new structural detail with no Parmar equivalent — supplementary, not conflicting.
  • Andaman & Nicobar's island count ("about 572") is a cross-book figure that varies by source/definition (some cite ~836 including islets/rocks) — not independently verified here, transcribed as printed.
  • Lucent's "Mountain Peaks of Andaman & Nicobar" table cites NCERT Class-XI (2023-24) directly and gives slightly different peak heights (Saddle Peak 738 m, Diavolo 515 m, Koyob 460 m) than Parmar's own map labels (732 m / 295 m / 495 m) — both retained above rather than silently unified, since the discrepancy is a genuine cross-source variance in a testable figure.

A.3 Doubts carried from the raw extraction

  • The West Coast islands list jumps from item 3 (Bet Dwarka) to item 5 (Alia Bet) with no item 4 printed — transcribed exactly as printed, not renumbered; a bullet may have been dropped during the source's own scanning/printing.
  • "Marusthali" is translated as "Land of Dead" (not "Land of the Dead") — transcribed as printed.
  • The Thar Desert's rain-shadow explanation ("of the Bay of Bengal current," Lucent) is non-standard phrasing — see the DOUBT box in §4.

A.4 Completeness check

Every fact in northern_plains_and_islands_raw_extraction.md is represented above except: decorative map labels with no independent testable content (the physiographic-divisions diagram; the detailed Andaman & Nicobar place-name map labels beyond what's captured in §7 — Mayabander, Pahalgoon, Baratang, Herbertabad, Aberdeen, Kakana, Tarasa Dwip, Bananga, etc. — settlement/place names with no attached fact); and the "India — Outline Map" port-location plotting, whose content is already captured in the §3 ports table.

5 · Climate of India

SSC CGL Tier I · Geography · keyword revision notes · seasons & monsoon mechanics → rainfall distribution → ENSO/IOD → Köppen classification → winds, jet streams & cyclones → ocean currents · appendix at end

Last-minute 12

  1. India's climate = 4 seasons: Winter (Dec-Feb) · Summer (Mar-May) · Monsoon/SW (Jun-Sep) · Retreating/NE monsoon (Oct-Nov)
  2. Monsoon = seasonal wind reversal (Arabic "Mausim") ★: summer → sea-to-land, heavy rain; winter → land-to-sea, dry ★
  3. Classical monsoon theory = Edmund Halley's Thermal Theory ★ — differential land/sea heating drives the wind reversal (not the Arabian Sea or NE-monsoon branches specifically)
  4. Monsoon onset: Kerala, first week of June, Western Ghats (orographic) — splits into Arabian Sea + Bay of Bengal branches, meeting NW of the Ganga Valley/Punjab Plains
  5. Rain-shadow ★: Pune (leeward of Western Ghats) gets far less rain than windward Mumbai ★; Chota Nagpur Plateau is fed mainly by the Bay of Bengal branch (Arabian Sea branch weakens after crossing the Ghats) ★
  6. Mango Showers = pre-monsoon showers (Mar-May, Kerala/Karnataka) that ripen mangoes early ★; also Kal Baisakhi/Nor'westers (WB, jute/tea) and Blossom Showers (Kerala, coffee)
  7. Convectional rainfall is most intense over oceans ★ (highest moisture availability) — not deserts, plateaus, or leeward slopes
  8. Weather elements = temperature/humidity/wind/rainfall; soil pH is NOT a weather element ★ (it's a soil property)
  9. Continentality: coastal cities (Mumbai) have the least annual temperature range ★ — inland cities (Delhi/Bhopal/Srinagar) swing more between summer & winter
  10. Thar/Indian Desert (Köppen climate angle) ★: lies west of the Aravallis, sandy plain with Barchan dunes, <150 mm rain/yr, ephemeral streams that never reach the sea, spreads across Rajasthan/Gujarat/Punjab/Haryana — full physical-geography detail already in Batch 4 §4
  11. Köppen (1884): 5 groups A/B/C/D/E; mid-latitude cold desert = BWk ★ (B=dry, W=desert, k=cold winter) vs hot desert = BWh (e.g. Sahara)
  12. Cyclones: Bay of Bengal > Arabian Sea in frequency (funnel shape, more heat/freshwater); rotate anti-clockwise (N. hemisphere)/clockwise (S.) — opposite for anticyclones

Key

TRAPeasily confused pair
PYQa real past SSC paper tested this — exact citation given
HOOKmemory aid — added by Claude, not from sources
DOUBTsources conflict / unverified
★ tested in geography_pyq.pdf (exam+date+shift in the appendix) · no personal attempt data exists for this source, so there are no "my mistake" boxes anywhere in this build
ITCZ Intertropical Convergence Zone · MJO Madden-Julian Oscillation · IOD Indian Ocean Dipole · ENSO El Niño Southern Oscillation

1Factors affecting climate & Climate vs Weather

  • 8 factors: Latitude (Tropic of Cancer splits tropical/subtropical) · Altitude (↑height → thinner air, lower temp/pressure) · Himalayas (block cold winds, shape monsoon) · Land-water distribution (differential heating) · Distance from sea (coastal=moderate, inland=extreme) · Pressure/wind systems · Relief (orographic rain, plateau wind effects) · Ocean currents
ClimateAverage weather over 30+ years
WeatherDay-to-day short-term conditions
TRAPWeather elements = temperature, humidity, wind, rainfall ★ — soil pH is not one of them; it's a soil property, not an atmospheric one. A PYQ tested exactly this.

2The four seasons

SeasonMonthsKey mechanics
WinterDec-FebCold dry Arctic/Central-Asian winds, blocked partly by Himalayas. Jet streams (upper troposphere, 9-13 km) bring Western Disturbances (from Mediterranean/Caspian Sea, called "Mahawat" in Rajasthan) → beneficial Rabi-crop winter rain. Rising night temps in N. India signal WD arrival.
SummerMar-MayITCZ shifts to 20-25°N (over the Gangetic plain, called the monsoon trough) by July → thermal low over NW India. Tibetan Plateau heating + easterly jet streams (13-14°N) + high pressure east of Madagascar all help pull in the monsoon. Pre-monsoon showers: Nor'westers/Kal Baisakhi (WB, jute+tea) & Bordoisila (Assam); Blossom Showers (Kerala, coffee); Mango Showers (coastal Karnataka/Kerala) ★
SW MonsoonJun-Sep"Monsoon" ← Arabic Mausim (seasonal wind reversal). Onset: Kerala, 1st week June (orographic, Western Ghats). Splits into Arabian Sea + Bay of Bengal branches, meeting NW of Ganga Valley/Punjab Plains. Break in monsoon = temporary rainfall lull.
Retreating/NE MonsoonOct-NovWithdrawal begins from the NW; passing over the Bay of Bengal causes cyclones + heavy Coromandel Coast (TN) rain. "October heat" = oppressive post-retreat humidity/heat. Leh (Himalayan rain-shadow) gets least rainfall.
HOOKEdmund Halley's Thermal Theory ★ = the classical explanation for monsoon reversal — summer land-heating creates low pressure → sea-to-land moist winds → rain; winter land-cooling creates high pressure → land-to-sea dry winds. A PYQ asked which branch this theory refers to — the answer is none of the specific branches, it's the general thermal mechanism.

3Rainfall distribution across India

HighWestern side of Western Ghats, Meghalaya, Brahmaputra Valley (incl. Mawsynram/Cherrapunji, Khasi Hills)
MediumE. Tamil Nadu, NE Peninsula, parts of MP, Cachar Valley (Assam)
LowW. UP, Delhi, Haryana, Punjab, J&K, E. Rajasthan
InadequateW. Rajasthan (leeward of Aravallis), Ladakh
  • Chota Nagpur Plateau gets most of its rain from the Bay of Bengal branch ★ (lies in its direct path) — the Arabian Sea branch has already weakened crossing the Western Ghats
  • Convectional rainfall is most intense over oceans ★ — highest moisture availability drives strong convection/condensation
  • Mumbai (coastal) shows the least hot-cold temperature swing ★ vs inland Delhi/Bhopal/Srinagar — the sea's moderating effect (see Continentality, §6)

4La Niña / El Niño (ENSO)

FeatureLa NiñaEl Niño
Meaning"Small girl""Child Christ / small boy"
Sea surface tempBelow average, east-central equatorial Pacific (cold ENSO phase)Above average, east-central equatorial Pacific (warm ENSO phase)
Trade windsStrengthen — push warm water westWeaken — warm water spreads east
Tahiti-Darwin pressurePositive (Tahiti > Darwin)Negative (Darwin > Tahiti)
Effect on IndiaIntense rain/floods (also Australia); drier South AmericaDroughts in Asia/Australia/US; India — below-average, late monsoon
S. America fishingFlourishesWon't flourish

5Temperature/rainfall variation terms, Continentality, MJO & IOD

  • Diurnal Temperature Range: highest−lowest temp in 24 hrs. Annual Temperature Range: hottest-month avg − coldest-month avg. Annual Rainfall Variation: rainfall difference across regions/years
  • Continentality effect ★: distance-from-sea → more extreme climate. Coastal (Mumbai/Chennai/Bengaluru) = moderate; inland (Kanpur/Delhi/Jalandhar) = extreme
  • MJO (Madden-Julian Oscillation): eastward-moving planetary cloud/rainfall waves circling the equator every 30-60 days
  • Indian Ocean Dipole (IOD): SST difference between the ocean's west & east poles — Positive IOD → good India rainfall; Negative IOD → reduced rain chance for India, increased for Australia

6Köppen Climate Classification (1884) & the Thar Desert's climate code

CodeGroupCriterion
ATropicalColdest month avg ≥ 18°C
BDryPotential evaporation > precipitation
CWarm TemperateColdest month avg between −3°C and 18°C
DCold Snow ForestColdest month avg ≤ −3°C
EPolar/TundraAll months avg < 10°C

Climate codes: S=Steppe · W=Desert · m=Monsoon · f=Full rainfall · w=Winter dry · s=Summer dry · h=hot · k=cold

HOOKMid-latitude (cold) desert = BWk ★ (B=dry, W=desert, k=cold winter) — e.g. the Thar Desert. A hot/tropical desert (Sahara-type) = BWh. India's own Koeppen map also shows Dfc (cold snow forest), BShw, Cwg, Aw zones.
HOOKThar/Indian Desert — climate-classification facts (full physical geography in Batch 4 §4): lies west of the Aravalli Range ★ (not east — a PYQ tested this directly), an undulating sandy plain with crescent-shaped Barchan dunes ★, <150 mm rain/year, ephemeral streams that never reach the sea ★, spread across Rajasthan/Gujarat/Punjab/Haryana. Not a rocky/forested plateau, and not part of the Deccan's black-soil belt — both wrong-option traps in the same PYQ set.

7Wind systems — permanent winds, Coriolis force & ITCZ

7.1 Permanent (planetary) winds

  • Trade Winds: sub-tropical high → equatorial low, 30°N/S to equator; NE Trades (N. hemisphere), SE Trades (S.) — name derives from the nautical expression "to blow trade" (along a regular path)
  • Westerlies: 30°-60° both hemispheres, blow W→E; stronger/steadier in S. hemisphere (less landmass) — "Roaring Forties," "Furious Fifties," "Shrieking/Screaming Sixties"
  • Polar Easterlies: Polar High → Sub-polar Low, 60°-poles
  • The 3-cell circulation model, pole to equator: Polar Cell (Polar Easterlies) → Ferrel Cell (Westerlies) → Hadley Cell (Trade Winds), meeting at the Equatorial Low/Doldrums
  • Wind measurement: direction = Wind Vane, speed = Anemometer, humidity = Hygrometer
  • Coriolis Force: deflects wind right (N. hemisphere)/left (S.); zero at equator, max at poles; stronger with wind speed
  • Geostrophic Wind: pressure-gradient force balances Coriolis force → wind flows parallel to isobars

7.2 ITCZ, Doldrums & Horse Latitudes

  • ITCZ: where NE & SE trade winds converge; between the Tropics; sits at 20-25°N in July. Moves more over land than sea (land heats/cools faster)
  • Doldrums: calm low-pressure equatorial belt (10°N-10°S), overlaps the ITCZ
  • Horse Latitudes: ~30°N/S, subtropical high, calm/dry/sunny — where trade winds & westerlies diverge

7.3 Jet Streams — Lucent's 4 types (detailed, testable figures)

Polar NightBeyond 60°N/S, both hemispheres
Polar Frontal30°-70°N, 9-12 km height — a.k.a. Rossby Waves (after scientist Carl-Gustaf Rossby)
Sub-Tropical Westerly20°-35°N, 10-14 km — brings India's winter Western Disturbances (Dec-Feb)
Tropical Easterly25°-35°N, 14-16 km, 100-150 mb — drives the Indian monsoon burst (warm jet lifts hot/humid air → convectional rain)

General jet-stream speed: 150-200 km/hr, with the core sometimes reaching 325 km/hr. Found mainly in the Northern Hemisphere; in the south, mainly over the South Pole region (light Rossby waves appear at other southern latitudes too).

8Local winds (world) & Land/Sea Breeze

WindCharacterWhere
ChinookWarm, dry ("snow eater")Eastern Rockies, Canada
FoehnWarm, dry (ripens grapes)Northern Alps
SiroccoWarm, dry ("blood rain")N. Africa → Mediterranean → Italy/Spain
KhamsinHot, dry, dustyEgypt
HarmattanDry, dusty, warm ("Dr. Wind"/"Doctor")Gulf of Guinea / W. Africa coast
LooHot, dry ("heat wave")Northern India
BrickfielderHot, dry, dustyAustralia
BlizzardVery cold, snow-bearingCanada & Siberia
MistralStrong, cold, dryFrance
BoraStrong, coldAdriatic Sea
PamperoColdPampas, Argentina
Santa AnaWarm, dryCalifornia, USA
SimoomWarm, dry, dust stormsArabian Desert
HOOKLand Breeze (night, land cools faster → land-to-sea, autumn/winter) vs Sea Breeze (day, land heats faster → sea-to-land, spring/summer) — opposite direction, opposite season-bias, mirrors the monsoon's own summer/winter reversal logic at a daily scale.

9Cyclones — tropical vs temperate, naming & anticyclones

  • Tropical cyclone formation: warm moist air rises over ocean (SST ≥27°C) → low pressure → Coriolis spin → strengthens with a pre-existing weak low. Rotates anti-clockwise (N. hemisphere), clockwise (S.)
  • Favourable conditions: high SST (27°C+), atmospheric instability, Coriolis force, pre-existing weak low
  • Temperate/extra-tropical cyclones: form via frontogenesis — polar-easterly cold air meets sub-tropical-westerly warm air (60-65° latitude), forming a front (they mix slowly since the air masses differ). The reverse process — a front weakening/dissolving — is frontolysis
  • The cyclone's "eye": abnormally high temperature and the lowest pressure point in the storm
FeatureTropicalTemperate
OriginOver seaOver sea & land
Area/destructionSmall area, major destructionLarge area, less destruction
FrequencyRareFrequent
MovementEast → WestWest → East
  • World names: Hurricane (Atlantic) · Typhoon (S. China Sea/W. Pacific) · Willy-Willy (Australia) · Cyclone (India) — named by regional committees (e.g. WMO/ESCAP panel, 13 countries, pre-approved sequential lists)
  • Bay of Bengal > Arabian Sea in cyclone frequency — reasons: landmass-surrounded (more heat transfer), funnel-shaped (wind converges from all sides), more freshwater influx, monsoonal trough
  • No cyclones in: South Atlantic & South-Eastern Pacific; none at the Equator (no Coriolis force there)
  • Anticyclone: high-pressure, sinking air, clear/dry/calm — clockwise (N. hemisphere), anti-clockwise (S.) — the reverse rotation of a cyclone

10Ocean currents

  • Movement causes: wind, density difference, gravity, Coriolis force, coastline shape
  • Thermohaline circulation ("global conveyor belt"): driven by temperature+salinity → density differences
  • Cold currents flow Poles→Equator; warm currents flow Equator→Poles
  • Gyre: circular ocean-current system, Coriolis-driven
HOOKCold currents — "BP GOLF World Cup California": Benguela, Peru/Humboldt, Greenland, Oyashio, Labrador, Falkland, World=W. Australian, Cup=Canary, California. Warm currents (no mnemonic in source): Brazil, Mozambique, Agulhas, Gulf Stream, Alaska, Norwegian, Kuroshio, E. Australia.
  • Effects: cold currents on tropical/sub-tropical west coasts → coastal deserts (cold water = cooler, drier air above it). Warm+cold currents meeting → best fishing zones (plankton) but also dangerous fog for harbours
  • Thermocline: depth below which temperature drops suddenly. Halocline: depth below which salinity rises rapidly
