1Geomorphology basics — Weathering, Mass Movement, Erosion & Denudation

  • "Father of Geomorphology" = William Morris Davis. All geomorphic processes stem from Endogenic (internal — diastrophism, volcanism) or Exogenic (external — powered by the Sun) forces. Himalayas rise because endogenic > exogenic; Aravallis shrink because exogenic > endogenic.
  • Weathering (in-situ breakdown, no movement): Physical (Unloading/Expansion, Frost Wedging — cold mountains, Salt Crystallisation — coasts/deserts) · Chemical (Solution — e.g. limestone's CaCO₃ dissolving; Hydration) · Biological (organisms — earthworms, roots). Effect: Exfoliation → can form a Tor (rounded granite hill).
  • Mass Movement (downslope movement under gravity — weathering NOT a prerequisite): Rapid (landslides, avalanches, earthflow, mudflow) · Slow (creep, solifluction).
  • Erosion: transportation of debris by running water/groundwater/glaciers/wind/waves. Denudation = the overall umbrella term for all wearing-away processes together.
TRAPThe PYQ book's own match-the-column question on this exact chain appeared twice with different labelling ★ — one version: Weathering→Gravitational/Molecular stress/Chemical actions (in-situ); Mass Movement→Gravitational force (downslope under gravity); Erosion&Transportation→Kinetic energy (agents carry material); Denudation→External forces acting on Earth's surface (the broad umbrella). A second version swapped some driving-force labels around (e.g. pairing Weathering with "Gravitational/Internal stress/Chemical reactions" instead). Both versions are transcribed faithfully in A.1 as separate citations rather than merged into one, since the PYQ book itself asks them as two distinct questions with slightly different option wording.
HOOKQuick distinguishing test: does the material actually MOVE? Weathering = no (in-situ). Mass Movement/Erosion = yes (downslope, or carried away).

2Landslide & mass-movement types

TypeMechanism
RockslideLarge coherent rock mass slides along a defined planar surface (fault/joint/bedding plane); affects considerable depth ★
Rock fallRock detaches and falls freely/bounces down a steep slope
SlumpRotational — material rotates backward along a concave slip surface; steep undercut slope; terrace-like hillside shape ★
Debris fallA mass of debris (soil+rock+organic matter) falls or slides down rapidly
TRAPOnly Slump is defined by backward rotation of the slipping mass — Rockslide, Rock fall and Debris fall are all straight/downward movements without this rotational signature. ★

3Landforms created by Rivers

  • A river passes through Youthful (upper stage, high speed, vertical erosion) → Mature (middle stage, high gradient to flat plain) → Old (lower stage, deposition-dominated) stages.
  • Erosional: V-shaped valley (vertical erosion) · Gorge (deep/narrow, hard rock) ★ · Canyon (gorge in layered sedimentary rock) · Waterfall (soft-rock erosion drop; the Plunge Pool is the deep hole scoured at its base) — sub-types: Cataract (hard+soft rock create an uneven, turbulent bed), Cascade (a multi-step series of small falls), Plunge (water drops straight down without touching the cliff face) · Potholes (swirling-pebble abrasion) · Rapids (alternating hard/soft rock) · Peneplain (near-level, fully eroded plain) · River terraces (former floodplains left elevated) · Entrenched meander (deep-cut meander in uplifted mountainous terrain).
  • Meanders ★: form via lateral erosion on the outer/concave bank + deposition on the inner/convex bank (point bar), once a river's speed drops on reaching flatter terrain.
  • Depositional: Flood plains (alluvium from repeated floods) · Natural levees (raised sediment banks — floodplains of large rivers, e.g. Mississippi, Huang-Ho, Ganga's southern bank) ★ · Alluvial fans (fan-deposit on leaving mountains) · Braided channels (heavy-sediment interweaving channels) · Ox-bow lakes (cut-off meander loop) · Delta (mouth distributaries; term coined by Herodotus from the Greek letter Δ; world's largest = the Ganges/Sunderbans Delta).
TRAPPYQ ★: in the alluvial plains of the Ganga, Rapids will NOT be found (they need steep-gradient, hard-rock, mountainous/youthful-stage rivers) — Oxbow lakes, meanders, and braided channels are all found there instead.
Farakka Barrage ★ — on the Ganga (Bhagirathi-Hooghly distributary), Murshidabad district, West Bengal, near the Bangladesh border; maintains Kolkata Port's navigability by increasing Hooghly River water supply. Cross-refer Batch 6 for the Ganga system's fuller build.
HOOKErosional vs Depositional, one-line test: erosional landforms are carved OUT of rock (valley, gorge, canyon); depositional landforms are BUILT UP from sediment (delta, levee, floodplain, fan).

4Landforms created by Glaciers

  • Erosional: Cirque (bowl-shaped hollow at glacier head; melts into a Tarn lake) · Arete (sharp ridge between two cirques) · Horn (pyramid peak from 3+ cirques) · U-shaped/Glacial Valley (wide, flat-bottomed, unlike a river's narrow V-shape) · Hanging Valley ★ (tributary-glacier valley left at a higher level, less eroded — often creates a waterfall; its spurs erode into Triangular Facets, aka "truncated spurs") · Fjord (sea-drowned glacial valley — Norwegian, S. Chilean coasts).
  • Depositional: Moraines (unsorted till along glacier sides — lateral/medial/terminal/ground) · Drumlins (oval till hills, "basket of eggs" topography; blunt stoss end faces ice-flow, tapered lee end trails it) · Outwash Plains (well-sorted meltwater-deposited sand/gravel beyond the glacier) · Eskers ★ (long sinuous ridges of sand/gravel from subglacial meltwater deposition — NOT ice-carved bedrock, NOT till pushed by stagnant ice).
TRAPPYQ ★ distinguishing Glacial Till from water-laid glacial sediment — Till is unsorted, unstratified, all particle sizes mixed, deposited directly by ice; glacio-fluvial (meltwater) sediment is layered and sorted instead.
Glaciers: Continental (vast ice sheets — Antarctica, Greenland) vs. Alpine/mountain (Himalaya/Alps/Andes/Rockies snow-fields sliding downslope). An Iceberg is a broken continental-glacier block floating in the sea (mostly submerged, since ice is only slightly less dense than water).

5Landforms created by Groundwater — Karst Topography

  • "Karst" comes from the Karst Plateau (south-western Slovenia/NE Italy). Formed by groundwater dissolving soluble rocks (limestone/dolomite, rich in CaCO₃). India's Karst regions: Madhya Pradesh, Andhra Pradesh, Telangana, Odisha, parts of the Himalayas.
  • Erosional: Sinkholes (surface depressions from limestone dissolution/collapse) · Swallow holes (streams vanish underground) · Dolines (enlarged sinkhole) · Uvalas (merged dolines) · Lapies (grooved/ridged limestone surface) · Limestone pavements (jointed flat limestone blocks) · Caves.
  • Depositional ★: Stalactite (hangs from the roof, calcium-carbonate drip deposit) · Stalagmite (rises from the floor) · Pillar/Column (formed when a stalactite and stalagmite meet/join).
TRAPPYQ ★ on cave features — correct pairing: Pillar = formed by the union of stalactite and stalagmite. Distractor swaps: "Stalactite rises from the floor" (wrong — it HANGS from the roof) and "Stalagmite hangs from the roof" (wrong — it RISES from the floor) are both reversed on purpose to trap the unwary.
Karst topography ★ is primarily caused by groundwater's chemical action (weathering + erosion) on soluble limestone/dolomite — not by river meandering, glacial deposition, or wind erosion, which are separate landform families entirely.

6Landforms created by Wind — Desert/Aeolian landforms

  • Erosional ★: Yardang (wind-parallel ridges/grooves) · Mushroom rocks/Pedestal rocks (narrow base, broad cap — softer lower rock erodes faster by wind abrasion) · Zeugen (tabular flat-top, steep-sided from differential erosion) · Mesa/Table rocks (broad flat top, WIDE uniform base — resembling a small plateau) · Inselberg/Monadnock (isolated residual hill in a pediplain, shaped by wind deflation+abrasion) · Playa (temporary desert lake bed, leaves salt/sediment) · Pediplain (broad eroded surface from merged pediments).
  • Depositional: Sand dunes/Loess (fine wind-blown sand/silt). Dune types: Barchan (crescent, wings point away from wind — Jaisalmer, Rajasthan) · Seif (Barchan with only one wing) · Parabolic (crescent facing opposite direction, vegetation-anchored) · Transverse (perpendicular to wind) · Longitudinal (parallel to wind). Oasis = small fertile water-source patch in arid land.
TRAPPYQ ★ on desert erosional-landform statements — Mushroom rocks are wind-formed (NOT water-formed) and found in deserts (NOT glacial valleys); Mesa/Table rocks have wide, uniform bases (NOT narrow bases); Pedestal rocks ARE correctly remnants shaped by wind deflation/abrasion. Statement-3-only was the correct answer in one framing of this question.
HOOKAll three — mushroom rocks, mesas, inselbergs/pedestals — are collectively "characteristic aeolian landforms of arid and semi-arid regions," per the PYQ's own explanation. ★

7Landforms created by Sea Waves

  • Erosional: Sea Cliff (steep coastal rock face from wave erosion) · Sea Cave (widened crack/hollow) · Sea Arch (wave-eroded headland opening) · Sea Stack (isolated pillar after an arch collapses).
  • Depositional: Beach (parallel sand/pebble deposit) · Spit (elongated sand ridge via longshore drift) · Bar (sand ridge across a bay/river mouth) · Barrier (offshore ridge parallel to coast) · Lagoon (shallow water cut off by a bar/spit — called Kayal in Kerala) · Tombolo (sand ridge connecting an island to the mainland) · Coastal sand dunes.
HOOKIndia's classic sea-cliff/sea-cave coast: Shrivandhan, Ratnagiri, Malvan, Vengurle (all Maharashtra's Konkan coast — cross-refer Batch 4).

8Mountains, Plateaus & Plains — typology (Lucent-only)

Mountain typeFormation & example
BlockMiddle lower (Rift Valley) — Vindhyachal/Satpura (India), Black Forest (Germany)
ResidualFrom weathering — Aravalli, Nilgiri, Parasnath
AccumulatedSand/soil/lava build-up — Sand Dunes
FoldInternal-motion folding — Himalayas, Alps, Andes, Rockies
Plains typeFormation & example
StructuralUplift+erosion of flat bedrock — Deccan Plateau (India), N. American Great Plains
ErosionalWind/water/glacier wear-down — Amazonian Plain
Depositional — AlluvialRiver deposits — Indo-Gangetic Plain
Depositional — LoessWind-deposited fine silt — the Pampas
HOOKPlateaus rise ≥300m with a flat/rough top — types include Intermountainous (Tibetan Plateau), Continental (formed via a surfaced laccolith), and Bank (ocean-edge) plateaus.

9India-specific PYQ facts & cross-references

  • Chambal Ravines ★ — deep, complex ravine terrain from rapid erosion along the Chambal River, spanning Rajasthan (Dholpur, Karauli), Madhya Pradesh (Morena, Bhind, Sheopur), and Uttar Pradesh (Etawah). Cross-refer Batch 6/9 for the Chambal river system itself.
  • Hesseraghatta Grassland ★: NW of Bengaluru, Karnataka — rich biodiversity site (Lesser Florican and other rare birds/butterflies); now the "Greater Hesseraghatta Grassland Conservation Reserve".
  • Kadbanwadi/Kadanwadi Grassland ★: in Maharashtra — an important natural grassland known for bird/grassland-organism biodiversity conservation.
HOOKThese two named grasslands (Hesseraghatta/Karnataka, Kadbanwadi/Maharashtra) sit awkwardly under this "Lithosphere" bucket topographically, but the PYQ book tests them here as landform/topography-adjacent facts — likely worth a second look if a future Wildlife Conservation or Ecosystem batch also touches grassland conservation, to cross-reference rather than duplicate.
The plate-tectonics + seafloor-spreading cause of continents/oceans' uneven distribution ★ is tested here for a third time across this build (also in Batches 15 and 16) — cited once more in A.1 since it recurs as a distinct question in this bucket too, but not rebuilt in the body beyond this cross-reference.

18 · Hydrosphere

SSC CGL Tier I · Geography · keyword revision notes · Continental Drift & Seafloor Spreading → Ocean-floor relief features → Continents of the World → Oceans of the World → Ocean Currents → Corals & reference facts · appendix at end

Last-minute 12

  1. Indian Ocean = the third-largest ocean in the world — order is Pacific > Atlantic > Indian > Arctic (smallest) ★
  2. Abyssal plains are vast, flat deep-ocean floors — TRUE. Oceanic trenches are NOT "shallow depressions" — they are the narrowest and deepest points of the ocean floor (e.g. Mariana Trench), formed by plate tectonics ★
  3. Earth's large-scale ocean currents are jointly driven by Wind (surface currents) and Density differences — temperature + salinity (deep-ocean currents) — NOT tides, salinity/temperature alone, or earthquakes/volcanoes ★
  4. Maldives = the smallest Indian Ocean island nation by area (≈300 sq km, 26 coral atolls) — also the smallest country in Asia ★
  5. Continental Drift Theory (Alfred Wegener, 1912): all continents were once joined as the supercontinent Pangaea, surrounded by one ocean, Panthalassa
  6. Seafloor Spreading Theory (Harry H. Hess, 1962): new oceanic crust forms at mid-ocean ridges (divergent boundaries) — so oceanic crust is younger than continental crust, and gets older with distance from the ridge
  7. Ocean-floor Major Relief Features, shallow → deep: Continental Shelf → Continental Slope → Continental Rise → Abyssal Plain
  8. Mariana Trench = deepest part of the Pacific Ocean (11 km / 11,034 m); Challenger Deep = the deepest known point within it
  9. Only ocean named after a country: Indian Ocean. Deepest & largest: Pacific. Shallowest, smallest & least saline: Arctic
  10. The Indian Ocean is the one exception to normal current behaviour — its currents reverse direction with the monsoon winds TRAP
  11. Africa is the only continent crossed by all three of the Tropic of Cancer, the Equator, and the Tropic of Capricorn
  12. Corals need saline, clear, sunlit water at 20°–32°C; live in symbiosis with Zooxanthella algae. Coral Bleaching = corals expel the algae (turning white) when water gets too warm — climate-change driven

1Continental Drift & Seafloor Spreading

  • Continental Drift Theory — Alfred Wegener, 1912. All of today's continents were once clumped together in a single supercontinent, Pangaea, surrounded by one water body, Panthalassa.
  • Evidences: the jigsaw-puzzle fit of coastlines; fossil deposits (Palaeontology = the study of fossils) and botanical evidence found on now-separated continents; rocks of the same age found across oceans; the Ghana gold deposit (a Placer deposit) — gold veins are found in Ghana/South Africa, but the deposit itself is geologically linked to the Brazilian Plateau, since the two landmasses once lay side by side; and Gondwana System sediments — glacially-deposited Tillite sediments from India have matching counterparts in six other southern landmasses: Africa, Australia, Antarctica, Madagascar, South America and the Falkland Islands.
  • Reasons for drift, as Wegener himself suggested: (1) Tidal force, (2) Pole-fleeing force. The reason accepted today came later — Arthur Holmes, 1930 — the Convection Current Theory: continental drift occurs due to tectonic-plate movement, driven by convection cells in the mantle.
  • Heat sources driving mantle convection (three): radioactive decay of elements; residual heat left over from Earth's formation (≈4.6 billion years ago, when gravitational energy converted to heat); and latent heat released as the Earth's core gradually solidifies.
HOOKWegener proposed that the continents drifted and even guessed at forces behind it (tidal/pole-fleeing) — but he got the driving mechanism wrong. Holmes (1930) is the one who correctly identified mantle convection as the real engine — a useful two-name TRAP.
REFIn 1596 — over 300 years before Wegener — Abraham Ortelius's work "Thesaurus Geographicus" had already suggested the continents were not always in their present locations.
REFMilankovitch Theory: describes the collective effect of cyclical changes in Earth's orbital movement on its climate over thousands of years — a separate (non-drift) theory appearing alongside Continental Drift in the source, included here for reference; not itself confirmed against a PYQ in this bucket.
SEAFLOOR SPREADINGHarry H. Hess, 1962: new oceanic crust forms at mid-ocean ridges, where a divergent plate boundary lets magma erupt and push the older crust away from the ridge on either side. Rocks equidistant from a mid-ocean ridge are of equal age, and age increases with distance from the ridge — so oceanic crust is younger than continental crust.

2Ocean-floor relief features

Feature (major, shallow→deep)Notes
Continental ShelfShallowest part of the ocean; gradient ≤1°
Continental SlopeGradient 2°–5°
Continental RiseGentler transition zone beyond the slope
Abyssal PlainVast, flat deep-sea plain ★
TRAPPYQ ★: "Abyssal plains are vast, flat deep-ocean floors" — TRUE. "Oceanic trenches are typically shallow depressions" — FALSE: trenches are the narrowest and deepest points of the ocean floor (e.g. the Mariana Trench), formed by plate-tectonic activity at convergent boundaries — the exact opposite of "shallow."
  • Minor Relief Features: Seamount (underwater mountain); Guyot (a flat-topped seamount); Mid-Ocean Ridge; Trench; Coral; Atoll.
  • The Mid-Atlantic Ridge runs along the floor of the Atlantic Ocean and is part of the longest relief feature on Earth — created by a divergent plate boundary (cross-refer §4, the Atlantic's continuing widening).
HOOKShelf → Slope → Rise → Plain, in that order, is exactly the path a sinking ship's debris would travel from the coast to the deep ocean floor — shallow-to-deep, edge-to-centre.

3Continents of the World

ContinentKey facts
AsiaLargest & most populous — 44.6 million sq km (≈30% of world land), ≈4,299 million people (≈60% of world population). Highest point Mt. Everest (8,850 m); lowest point the Dead Sea (396.8 m below sea level). Home to Lake Baikal (world's oldest/deepest/largest-by-volume freshwater lake) and the Sunderbans (largest delta). Cradle of the Mesopotamian, Indus & Chinese civilizations and of Judaism, Hinduism, Christianity, Islam, Buddhism, Jainism, Sikhism, Taoism, Shintoism & Zoroastrianism etc. Wholly in the Northern Hemisphere; spans 10°S–80°N and 25°E–170°E (an 11-hour time difference across it). Bounded by the Arctic, Pacific & Indian Oceans; separated from Europe by the Ural Mountains/River & Caspian Sea. Europe+Asia together = Eurasia.
EuropeMuch smaller than Asia, lies to its west; the Arctic Circle passes through it; bound by water on three sides.
Africa2nd largest continent. The only continent crossed by all three of the Tropic of Cancer, the Equator, and the Tropic of Capricorn ★. Home to the Sahara (world's largest hot desert) and the Nile (world's longest river). Bound by oceans/seas on all sides.
North America3rd largest. Linked to South America by the narrow Isthmus of Panama. Lies entirely in the Northern & Western Hemisphere; surrounded by the Arctic, Atlantic & Pacific Oceans.
South AmericaMostly in the Southern Hemisphere; Atlantic & Pacific to its east/west. Home to the Andes (world's longest mountain range) and the Amazon (world's largest river by volume).
AustraliaSmallest continent; lies entirely in the Southern Hemisphere; surrounded by ocean on all sides — called an "island continent."
Antarctica5th largest; isolated, zero permanent population, entirely snow-covered — the "White Continent." See box for discovery history & India's stations.
ANTARCTICAJames Cook (English) first attempted to reach it, crossing the Antarctic Circle but never landing on the mainland. Roald Amundsen (Norwegian) was the first to reach the South Pole (1911). The first Indian to reach Antarctica was Ramcharan Jee (1960); the first Indian to reach the South Pole was Dr. Giriraj Sirohi. India's Antarctic stations: Dakshin Gangotri (1983, since abandoned/buried under snow), Maitri (operational since 1988, Schirmacher Oasis), Bharati (Larsemann Hills, from 2012). Highest peak: Mt. Vinson Massif. Mt. Erebus = the continent's only active volcano. The seas surrounding it (parts of the Indian, Pacific & Atlantic Oceans) are together called the Southern Ocean/Antarctic Ocean. The phenomenon of ozone depletion was first discovered here, in 1985.
DOUBTThe source names the first person to reach mainland Antarctica (1820, ship "Vostok") as "Fabian Welling Shawson" — transcribed exactly as printed. This does not match the commonly known history, where the credited discoverer is usually Fabian Gottlieb von Bellingshausen (a Russian expedition, ships Vostok & Mirny) — likely a printing/OCR-adjacent name garble in the source. Not confidently corrected here; verify against a primary source before treating either name as settled.
HOOKAntarctica is also called the "Continent Dedicated to Science" — no permanent residents, no coastal plain, and the lowest temperature ever recorded on Earth (−93.2°C, 2010, East Antarctic Plateau) was measured there.

4Oceans of the World

OceanArea (approx.)Avg. depthRank / notes
Pacific≈16,62,40,000 sq km (≈166 million sq km)4,280 mLargest & deepest ocean ★. Named "Pacific" (calm/peaceful) by Ferdinand Magellan. Mariana Trench (11,034 m) within it is the world's deepest trench.
Atlantic≈8,24,40,000 sq km (without dependent seas); ≈10,64,60,000 sq km (with them)3,300 m2nd largest. Named after Atlas (Greek mythology). Busiest ocean for trade/commerce (links Western Europe & NE USA). Formed by a rift in Gondwanaland — still widening today.
Indian≈7,34,40,000 sq km3,960 m3rd largest ★. Only ocean named after a country. Deeper than the Atlantic.
Arctic≈1,40,90,000 sq km987 mSmallest, shallowest & least saline (30 ppt) ocean. North Pole lies at its centre; mostly ice-covered.
PYQ ★: "Which is the third-largest ocean?" → Indian Ocean (Pacific > Atlantic > Indian > Arctic).
  • A newer, fifth ocean — the Southern Ocean — has been named (per the US National Ocean Service/NOAA), comprising the southern parts of the Pacific, Atlantic & Indian Oceans around Antarctica.
  • Islands: Pacific islands are mostly of volcanic or coral origin. Atlantic's continental islands: Newfoundland, British Isles; volcanic: Cuba, Jamaica, Puerto Rico (Iceland = largest volcanic island). Indian Ocean's largest continental islands: Madagascar & Sri Lanka; its volcanic/coral-origin islands: Mauritius, Andaman & Nicobar, Seychelles, Maldives, Lakshadweep.
  • PYQ ★: Maldives — the smallest Indian Ocean island nation by area (≈300 sq km, 26 coral atolls) and the smallest country in Asia.
  • Biosphere = the part of Earth where life exists. Lithosphere (land) covers 29.22% of Earth's surface (≈14,90,41,182 sq km); Hydrosphere (water) covers 70.78% (≈36,10,59,226 sq km) — together with the atmosphere, these three make life on Earth possible.

5Ocean Currents

  • Ocean Current = a large body of water flowing in a definite direction with a given intensity. Two types: Hot/Warm Currents (flow from tropical/lower-latitude zones toward temperate/sub-polar/higher latitudes) and Cold Currents (flow from higher latitudes toward lower latitudes).
  • PYQ ★: the two primary factors jointly driving Earth's large-scale ocean currents are Wind (surface currents) and Density differences — driven by temperature and salinity together (deep-ocean currents) — not tides, not salinity/temperature in isolation, and not seismic/volcanic activity.
  • Indian Ocean exception TRAP: this is the only ocean where current direction reverses with the seasonal change in Monsoon Wind direction — elsewhere, hot currents generally flow toward cooler oceans and cold currents toward warmer ones.
Selected currentsNature
Gulf Stream, Kuroshio, North/South Equatorial, Brazilian, AgulhasWarm
Humboldt (Peruvian), Labrador, Canary, Benguela, FalklandCold
South-West Monsoon Current (Indian Ocean)Warm & unstable
North-East Monsoon Current (Indian Ocean)Cold & unstable
HOOKThe full Pacific/Atlantic/Indian Ocean current-nature tables in the source list over 25 named currents — condensed here to the warm/cold pattern and the Indian Ocean's monsoon-driven exception, since no located PYQ tests an individual current's name beyond this bucket's own questions (see A.4).

6Corals & reference facts

  • Corals: marine organisms called the "Rainforest of the Sea." They live in a symbiotic relationship with Zooxanthella algae — the coral secretes CaCO₃ to protect the algae, and in return the algae produce food for the coral. Corals live in colonies.
  • Favourable conditions for coral survival: saline water (cannot survive in fresh water); sunlight; clear water; moderate temperature, 20°–32°C.
  • Atoll: a ring-shaped coral island/reef surrounding a central lagoon, formed as coral grows around a sinking volcanic island. The Great Barrier Reef (Australia) is the world's largest barrier reef.
  • Coral Bleaching: when water gets too warm, corals expel their Zooxanthella algae, turning completely white — climate change is a major driver.
FACTSMariana Trench = deepest part of the Pacific Ocean (11 km); Challenger Deep = the deepest known point within it. Atlantic Ocean = the busiest ocean (trade/commerce). Sargasso Sea = a unique, borderless region within the Atlantic Ocean, home to the brown alga Sargassum; the Black Sea is also within the Atlantic Ocean system as described by this source.
HOOK"Rainforest of the Sea" (corals) pairs naturally with the actual rainforest biome — both are named for extraordinarily high biodiversity packed into a relatively small area.

A.4 Completeness check

This batch draws on `rocks_continents_oceans_raw_extraction.md` PART A (book p.15 Continents/Oceans/Seafloor Spreading, book p.16 Corals) and PART B (book pp.118–121, Continents of the World + Oceans on the Earth) in full. Deliberately condensed: the full 20+ row Pacific/Atlantic and Indian Ocean current-name tables are condensed to the warm/cold pattern plus the Indian Ocean's monsoon-reversal exception, since no located PYQ in this bucket names an individual current beyond what's already cited in A.1. Not covered anywhere in this build yet: Parmar's own book pp.13–14 opening material on Rock formation and classification (Petrology, Igneous/Sedimentary/Metamorphic Rock definitions and sub-types, Plutonic bodies — Batholith/Dyke/Sill/Laccolith/Lopolith/Phacolith, the Rock Cycle, and Acidic-vs-Basic Rocks) remains genuinely unbuilt across the whole Geography series — it doesn't fit this Hydrosphere bucket thematically, was not absorbed into Batch 16 (Earthquake & Volcano, which took only the Volcano portion of the same pages) or Batch 17 (Lithosphere, built entirely from a different raw file), and has no dedicated bucket in the 24-topic PYQ index. Flagged here transparently for a possible future addendum or folding into Batch 24 (Miscellaneous) if a matching PYQ turns up, rather than silently dropped.

19 · Atmosphere

SSC CGL Tier I · Geography · keyword revision notes · Atmosphere & its layers → Insolation, Heat Budget & Albedo → Humidity, Pressure & Water on Earth → Water Cycle, Precipitation & Condensation → Clouds & Rainfall types → Weather, Climate & storm terms · appendix at end

Last-minute 12

  1. Troposphere = the lowest atmospheric layer (avg. 13 km; 8 km at poles, 18 km at equator) — all weather phenomena (clouds, rain, storms, lightning) occur here ★
  2. The Ozone layer sits within the Stratosphere (≈15–35 km) — it absorbs harmful UV radiation, protecting Earth from cancer & ecological damage ★
  3. The ionosphere (mainly within the Thermosphere) is a layer of charged particles/ions — created when atmospheric gases are ionized by Solar radiation (UV + X-rays), not cosmic or gamma radiation ★
  4. Kármán Line = an imaginary boundary ≈100 km above sea level marking where Earth's atmosphere ends and outer space begins ★
  5. Nitrogen (≈78%) and Oxygen (≈21%) are the two dominant gases in Earth's atmosphere ★
  6. Auroras near the poles form when the solar wind interacts with Earth's magnetic field, causing charged particles to collide with upper-atmosphere gases and emit light ★
  7. TRAP ★: clouds form in the Troposphere, NOT the Exosphere — the Exosphere is the outermost layer, almost devoid of gas, where satellites/ISS orbit
  8. Lapse rate in the Troposphere: temperature falls ≈1°C per 165 m (≈6.5°C/km). In the Mesosphere, temperature keeps falling to as low as ≈−100°C by ~80 km — the coldest part of the atmosphere ★
  9. Change of state at atmospheric pressure: Liquid→Solid = Solidification; Solid→Gas (direct) = Sublimation; Gas→Solid (direct) = Deposition; Liquid→Gas (slow) = Evaporation ★
  10. Evaporation rate depends on surface area, wind speed and humidity — NOT Buoyancy, which is an unrelated upward force in liquids/gases ★
  11. Convectional rainfall: surface heating → warm moist air rises → cools → condenses → rain — most common in the equatorial belt/Doldrums ★
  12. Waterspouts = rotating columns of air over water, generally less dangerous than land tornadoes ★

1Atmosphere & its evolution

  • Atmosphere: a mixture of gases surrounding the Earth, held in place by Earth's gravity; extends to ≈1,000 km, but 99% of its mass lies within just 32 km of the surface.
  • Evolution of the Atmosphere (3 stages): (1) Loss of the Primordial Atmosphere — the original H₂/He atmosphere was stripped away by excessive solar flares; (2) Volcanism (Degassing) — Earth's hot interior released gases via volcanic eruptions, forming a new atmosphere; (3) Biological Modification — plants/living organisms further transformed the atmosphere through photosynthesis.
  • Composition (by volume): Nitrogen (N₂) 78%, Oxygen (O₂) ≈21% ★, Argon (Ar) ≈0.9%, Carbon dioxide (CO₂) ≈0.04%, Neon, Helium, Ozone, Hydrogen — all in trace amounts. CO₂ and water vapour both absorb heat, keeping the atmosphere warm; water vapour also varies most (0–4% by volume) and is the source of all clouds/precipitation.
PYQ ★: "Which two gases dominate Earth's surface atmosphere?" → Nitrogen (N₂) and Oxygen (O₂) — not Argon/Neon or Oxygen/Neon.
PYQ ★: "Oxygen concentration in Earth's atmosphere is roughly?" → ≈21%, the second most abundant gas after Nitrogen (≈78%) — essential for respiration, found in maximum concentration in the Troposphere.

2Layers of the Atmosphere

LayerKey facts
TroposphereLowest layer ★. Height: 8 km (poles)–18 km (equator), avg. 13 km. Temperature decreases with height (Normal/Positive Lapse Rate: ≈1°C/165 m, ≈6.5°C/km) — at the Tropopause itself, temperature is ≈−80°C over the Equator vs. ≈−45°C over the Poles. Contains >90% of atmospheric mass & almost all water vapour. All weather occurs here ★. Ends at the Tropopause.
StratosphereExtends tropopause → ≈50 km. Contains the Ozone layer (≈15–35 km) ★, which absorbs harmful UV-B/UV-C radiation (releasing heat, so temperature increases with altitude here — a stable thermal inversion). UV-A (less harmful) mostly passes through. Cloud-free — ideal for jet-plane flight. Ends at the Stratopause.
MesosphereUp to ≈80–85 km. Coldest layer — temperature falls with height, to ≈−90°C to −100°C near its top ★. Meteors burn up here (air dense enough to compress/heat them). Ends at the Mesopause.
Thermosphere≈80–400+ km. Hottest layer — temperature increases sharply with altitude, since gas molecules absorb incoming UV & X-ray radiation ★, splitting into ions (hence also called the ionosphere ★). Reflects radio waves back to Earth (aids long-distance communication); protects Earth by burning up meteors/debris.
ExosphereOutermost, 5th layer — mostly Hydrogen & Helium, gas molecules sparse enough to escape gravity. Gradually merges into interplanetary space. No clouds form here ★ (that's the Troposphere's job) — satellites/the ISS orbit within/near this layer.
PYQ ★: "Which matched pair is incorrect?" → Exosphere – tropospheric clouds is the wrong pairing (clouds form in the Troposphere, not the Exosphere); Mesosphere–meteors-burn, Stratosphere–ozone layer, and Thermosphere–ISS orbit are all correctly matched.
KÁRMÁN LINE ★: an imaginary boundary ≈100 km (≈62 miles) above sea level, recognized as the line separating Earth's atmosphere from outer space.
HOOKAuroras ★ (not in either source book — PYQ-book only fact): near Earth's poles, solar-wind charged particles guided by Earth's magnetic field collide with upper-atmosphere gases, which emit coloured light as a result — the same charged-particle physics that makes the Thermosphere the ionosphere.
OZONEWorld Ozone Day: 16 September, marking the 1987 Montreal Protocol (Montreal, Canada) to phase out ozone-depleting CFCs. Ozone-layer thickness is measured in Dobson units. The Kigali Amendment targets phasing out HFCs.

3Insolation, Heat Budget & Temperature Inversion

  • Insolation = incoming solar radiation, received as short waves. The atmosphere doesn't heat directly from insolation — Earth's surface absorbs it and re-emits long-wave terrestrial radiation, which warms the air near the surface (why temperature falls with altitude in the Troposphere).
  • Heat transfer mechanisms: Conduction (molecule-to-molecule, no air movement) · Convection (vertical heat transfer via air-mass movement) · Advection (horizontal heat transfer via air movement — e.g. the Loo wind).
  • Heat Budget: of 100% incoming solar energy, ≈35% is reflected back to space; of the ≈65% absorbed, ≈14% is absorbed by the atmosphere and ≈51% by Earth's surface.
  • Temperature Inversion: temperature increases with height instead of decreasing → atmospheric stability → no rain. Favoured by: long winter nights (rapid surface heat loss), still air (no mixing), and clear cloudless skies (enhanced radiative cooling).
SurfaceAlbedo (%)
Asphalt (lowest)5–10
Oceans/Lakes8
Land surfaces14–18
Sand/Desert27
Sea ice35
Ice & snow50–70
Fresh snow (highest)80
ALBEDOProportion of incident radiation reflected by a surface — highest for ice caps/glaciers/fresh snow, lowest for dark surfaces like asphalt. Clouds have their own albedo range too: shallow broken clouds ≈30%, thicker mid/high clouds ≈35–68%, dense Cumulus ≈75%, Nimbostratus ≈85%, Cumulonimbus ≈90% (highest of all). Key greenhouse gases: water vapour, CO₂, CH₄ (Methane), N₂O (Nitrous Oxide), O₃ — NO₂ and SO₂ are NOT greenhouse gases.

4Humidity, Pressure & Water on Earth

  • Humidity = water vapour content of air (up to ≈4% by volume), expressed in three forms: Absolute Humidity (total moisture mass per unit air volume, gm/m³ — higher in warm air); Specific Humidity (mass of vapour, in grams, per kg of air); Relative Humidity (actual moisture ÷ maximum capacity, as %; at 100% RH, condensation/precipitation begins).
  • Dew Point: the temperature at which air becomes fully saturated, causing vapour to condense into liquid.
  • Atmospheric Pressure: pressure at a point due to the weight of the air column above it. Standard sea-level pressure ≈76 cm (29.92 in) of Mercury.
  • PYQ ★: "Which device is commonly used to measure temperature?" → Thermometer (Barometer measures pressure; Altimeter measures altitude; Speedometer measures vehicle speed).
  • Latent Heat of Vaporization: the heat energy needed to change a unit mass of liquid into gas at constant temperature.
  • Pressure-measuring instruments: Mercurial/Fortin's Barometer · Aneroid Barometer · Altimeter (Altitude Barometer) · Barograph (auto-recording Aneroid Barometer) · Microbarometer · Microbarovariograph. Pressure is also expressed in millibars (1 bar = 1,000 mb), now called hectopascals.
  • Water on Earth: 97.2% saline, only 2.8% fresh (per one source box). Of freshwater: ≈68.7% locked in glaciers/ice caps, ≈30.1% groundwater, the small remainder in lakes/rivers/atmosphere. Overall: 71% of Earth's surface is water, 29% is land.
HOOKThe pressure-instrument list (this section) and the wind-instrument pair (Wind Vane = direction, Anemometer = speed) are both cross-referenced from Batch 5 (Climate of India), which already built out Trade Winds/Westerlies/Jet Streams/Local Winds from the same raw file — not repeated here to avoid duplication.

5Water Cycle, Precipitation & Condensation

  • Water Cycle stages: Evaporation → Condensation → Transpiration → Precipitation → Surface flow → Groundwater.
  • Types of Precipitation: Rain (liquid, above freezing) · Snow (frozen crystals, below freezing) · Sleet (raindrops that freeze before landing) · Hail (ice balls/lumps, concentric rings visible).
  • Condensation forms: Dew (moisture condensing on surfaces) · Fog (ground-level cloud from temperature inversion, visibility <1 km) · Mist (more moisture than fog is wrong — mist has visibility >1 km, i.e. thinner than fog) · Frost (vapour → solid ice directly, skipping liquid). Radiation fog forms over valleys/low-lying land as the ground cools overnight; Advection fog forms when warm moist air moves over a cooler surface (coasts/plains). Smog = smoke + fog.
PYQ ★: change-of-state terms at atmospheric pressure — Liquid→Solid = Solidification (the tested answer); Gas→Solid direct = Deposition; Solid→Gas direct = Sublimation; Liquid→Gas (slow) = Evaporation.
PYQ ★: "NOT a main factor affecting the rate of evaporation" → Buoyancy (an upward force in liquids/gases, unrelated to evaporation) — Surface area, Wind speed and Humidity ARE genuine factors.
HOOKCloud seeding: aircraft disperse silver iodide/dry ice/salts into clouds to trigger extra condensation and induce rain/snow artificially.

6Clouds & Rainfall types

Cloud levelTypes & features
High (≈5–13 km)Cirrus (feathery, no rain, cyclone indicator) · Cirrocumulus · Cirrostratus (halo around sun/moon, signals an approaching cyclone)
Middle (≈2–7 km)Altostratus (widespread continuous rain when thick) · Altocumulus ("Sheep clouds")
Low (0–2 km)Stratus (fog-like) · Cumulostratus (light grey, fair-weather, common in winter) · Nimbostratus (dark, subtropical-cyclone rain) · Cumulus (fair weather) · Cumulonimbus (anvil-top, thunderstorms — the tropical/temperate-cyclone rain-bearer)
  • Rainfall types: Convectional — surface heating → rising, cooling moist air → condensation → rain; typical of the equatorial belt/Doldrums ★. Orographic/Relief — moist air forced up a mountain slope; Windward side (facing the wind, anabatic winds) gets heavy rain, e.g. Mumbai/Mangalore on the Western Ghats' western face; Leeward side (rain-shadow, katabatic winds) stays dry, e.g. Vidarbha/Bangalore. Cyclonic/Frontal — warm moist air meets cold dry air at a front.
HOOKThe Windward/Leeward Western Ghats example here is the same fact family already built with full India-specific detail in Batches 2/3/5 — cross-referenced, not rebuilt.

7Weather, Climate & storm-related terms

  • Weather = atmospheric conditions of a place at a given time, short-term. Climate = the integrated/averaged picture of weather over a long period — classically calculated over 30 years (WMO standard), though 35-year averages are also used.
  • PYQ ★: climate zones — tropical zones (near the equator, vertical sun-rays year-round) have minimal seasonal temperature variation; temperate zones (with inclined, seasonally-varying sun-rays) show four distinct seasons — both statements are correct.
  • PYQ ★: for southern India specifically — its diurnal & annual temperature range is actually low (tropical, maritime-moderated), so a statement claiming a high diurnal range there is incorrect; similarly, coastal regions have a lower annual range than continental/inland regions (not "greater," as a distractor statement claimed) — both such statements are incorrect.
  • PYQ ★: the BWhw Köppen code (B=Dry, W=Desert, h=Hot, w=Dry winter) matches Rajasthan's Thar Desert — cross-refer Batch 5 for the fuller Thar Desert / Köppen-classification treatment (built there via a different code, BWk, for the same broad desert-climate family).
HOOKWaterspouts ★ (PYQ-book-only fact, not in either source book): rotating columns of air forming over bodies of water (sea/lakes), cyclonic in motion, generally less dangerous than land tornadoes.
MISSIONThe National Mission on Sustainable Habitat (NMSH) ★ aims to develop climate-resilient cities in India via energy-efficient urban planning/waste management — one of the eight missions under the National Action Plan on Climate Change (NAPCC).
HOOKThe Tamil-Nadu-coast-stays-dry-in-SW-monsoon fact (its coastline runs parallel to the Bay-of-Bengal branch of the monsoon, so it misses that moisture — and instead gets its main rain from the retreating/NE monsoon) and the retreating-monsoon Assertion-Reason pair (low pressure over the Northern Plains replaced by high pressure → dry, clear weather, "October heat") both build on the monsoon mechanics already established in Batch 5 — cross-referenced rather than rebuilt in full here.

20 · Conservation of Natural Resources

SSC CGL Tier I · Geography · keyword revision notes · Forest Acts & Protected/Reserved Forests → Biosphere Reserves → Ramsar Wetlands → Project Tiger & National Parks → Protected Areas of India → Recent conservation schemes (2023–25) → Other current facts · appendix at end

Last-minute 12

  1. Indian Forest Act, 1878: in Reserved Forests, local communities' traditional rights were almost entirely eliminated; in Protected Forests, limited/controlled rights (grazing, fuel-wood collection) were still permitted ★
  2. Forest (Conservation) Amendment Act, 2023 exempts forest land ≤10 hectares near international borders (strategic/security projects, within 100 km) and ≤5 hectares for public-utility projects in LWE-affected areas — it does not exempt land recorded as forest in government records as of 25 October 1980 (that remains covered) ★
  3. India has 18 Biosphere Reserves, but only 12 of them (incl. Gulf of Mannar & Great Nicobar) are internationally recognized under UNESCO's World Network of Biosphere Reserves (WNBR) — Biosphere Reserves and World Heritage Sites are two distinct UNESCO programmes ★
  4. A Biosphere Reserve's three zones, innermost→outermost: Core (strictly protected) → Buffer (limited human activity) → Transition (sustainable development & settlements) ★
  5. Sundarbans = world's largest mangrove forest/delta, split ≈40% India (West Bengal) / ≈60% Bangladesh, formed at the Ganga-Brahmaputra-Meghna confluence; a UNESCO World Heritage Site since 1987, and habitat of the Royal Bengal Tiger ★
  6. Keibul Lamjao National Park (Manipur, part of Loktak Lake) = the world's only floating national park — its floating vegetation islands are called Phumdis ★
  7. Aravalli Green Wall Project (launched 2023): a green belt ≈5 km wide around the Aravalli range, across 29 districts of 4 states (Rajasthan, Haryana, Gujarat, Delhi) — aims to check desertification & restore degraded forest, not to build eco-sensitive highways ★
  8. Project Tiger (launched 1973): grew from 9 reserves → 58 reserves, now covering ≈2.3% of India's land area (84,487.83 km²) ★
  9. Project Cheetah = the world's first inter-continental large-carnivore translocation project — cheetahs brought from Namibia/South Africa to Kuno National Park (Madhya Pradesh); cheetahs had gone extinct in India in 1952 ★
  10. Green Credit Programme (under the Environment (Protection) Act, 1986): rewards voluntary environmental actions by individuals/entities — it is not a penalty scheme ★
  11. India's new Eco-Tourism Policy for protected areas: aims to balance conservation with local livelihoods via low-impact tourism — not to ban human activity or maximise profit ★
  12. Ramgarh Vishdhari Tiger Reserve (Rajasthan) and Simlipal National Park (Odisha, declared India's 107th National Park) are the two most recent milestone additions in this bucket's PYQs ★

1Forest Acts & Protected/Reserved Forests

  • Indian Forest Act, 1878 ★: categorized forests into Reserved and Protected Forests. In Reserved Forests, the traditional rights of local communities were almost entirely eliminated; in Protected Forests, limited and controlled rights (such as grazing and fuel-wood collection) were still permitted — the key distinction the exam tests.
  • Forest (Conservation) Amendment Act, 2023 ★: exempts certain categories of forest land from the Act's strict restrictions — up to 10 hectares for strategic/security projects within 100 km of international borders, and up to 5 hectares for public-utility projects in Left-Wing-Extremism (LWE) affected areas. NOT exempted: land recorded as forest in government records as of 25 October 1980, land notified as forest under the Indian Forest Act, 1927, and land proposed for silvicultural operations in reserved forests — these remain fully covered by the Act.
HOOKBoth Forest Acts (1878 and the 2023 Amendment) are PYQ-book-only facts — neither Parmar's nor Lucent's own chapters cover this specific legal/policy history in the pages read for this build.

2Biosphere Reserves in India

  • Biosphere Reserve: a protected-area category where people are an integral component, launched 1975 under UNESCO's Man and Biosphere (MAB) Programme. Functions: Conservation, Development (sustainable economic activity), and Scientific research/education.
  • Zonation ★: Core/Natural Zone (innermost, strictly protected, least-disturbed ecosystem) → Buffer Zone (research/education, limited human activity) → Transition Zone (outermost, active co-operation with local people — settlements, cropping, forestry continue in harmony with conservation).
  • India has 18 Biosphere Reserves (of 738 worldwide, across 134 countries) — but only 12 are internationally recognized under UNESCO's World Network of Biosphere Reserves (WNBR) ★, including Gulf of Mannar (Tamil Nadu) and Great Nicobar (Andaman & Nicobar). Nilgiri Biosphere Reserve (Tamil Nadu/Kerala/Karnataka, 1986) was India's first.
Reserve / StateEst.Notable fauna
Nilgiri (TN/Kerala/Karnataka)1986Nilgiri Tahr, Lion-tailed Macaque — India's first
Sundarban (WB)1989Royal Bengal Tiger
Gulf of Mannar (TN)1989Dugong/Sea cow
Great Nicobar (A&N)1989Saltwater Crocodile
Nanda Devi (Uttarakhand)1988Western Himalayan
Simlipal (Odisha)1994Gaur, Royal Bengal Tiger, Wild Elephant
Great Rann of Kutch (Gujarat)2008Indian Wild Ass — largest by area (12,454 km²)
PYQ ★: statement check — "All biosphere reserves are notified as UNESCO World Heritage Sites" is FALSE (Biosphere Reserves and World Heritage Sites are separate UNESCO programmes; only a few areas fall under both); "Gulf of Mannar and Great Nicobar are in the WNBR" is TRUE; "Biosphere reserves include core/buffer/transition zones" is TRUE.

3Ramsar Wetland Sites

  • Ramsar Convention (Iran, 1971; in force 1975): international treaty for conserving wetlands of ecological, economic, cultural, scientific and recreational value. India is a signatory since 1982.
  • India has designated 89 wetlands as Ramsar Sites (as of February 2025) — Tamil Nadu (20 sites) and Uttar Pradesh (10) lead the count, giving India the highest number of Ramsar sites in South Asia ★. Sites span both coastal and inland regions (e.g. Tso Kar, Ladakh — inland) — not exclusively coastal.
  • Recent (Feb 2025) additions include Sakkarakottai and Therthangal Bird Sanctuaries (Ramanathapuram district, Tamil Nadu) ★, Khachoedpalri Wetland (Sikkim), and Udhwa Lake Bird Sanctuary (Jharkhand).
PYQ ★: "The two bird sanctuaries Sakkarakottai and Therthangal are in which state?" → Tamil Nadu — important Ramanathapuram-district wetlands, granted Ramsar status in February 2025.
PYQ ★: statement check on Ramsar Sites — "India has the highest number of Ramsar sites in South Asia" TRUE; "Keibul Lamjao is the only floating National Park in the world and is also a Ramsar site" TRUE; "All Ramsar sites in India are in coastal regions" FALSE (Tso Kar, Ladakh, is inland) — so only statements 1 and 2 are correct.

4Project Tiger, Tiger Reserves & National Parks

  • Project Tiger ★: launched 1973 to save the endangered tiger. Grew from 9 reserves (1973-74) → 58 reserves (as on 16 May 2025), total area ≈84,487.83 km² — ≈2.3% of India's land area.
  • Ramgarh Vishdhari Tiger Reserve ★ — Bundi district, Rajasthan; declared 2022; serves as a wildlife corridor between Ranthambore and Mukundra Hills Tiger Reserves.
  • Simlipal (Odisha) ★ has been declared India's 107th National Park — already a Tiger Reserve and part of UNESCO's WNBR, famous for tigers and elephants.
  • Jim Corbett National Park (originally Hailey National Park, established 1936; renamed Ramganga National Park in 1952, then Jim Corbett in 1956) = India's first National Park — the world's first was Yellowstone (USA, 1872).
HOOKProject Cheetah ★: the world's first inter-continental large wild-carnivore translocation project — cheetahs brought from Namibia & South Africa, reintroduced in Kuno National Park (Madhya Pradesh); historic because cheetahs had gone extinct in India in 1952.
HOOKTrailGuard AI ★: an AI-enabled camera-sensor anti-poaching system, deployed in Simlipal Tiger Reserve (Odisha) — sends real-time alerts on human intrusion, boosting patrol effectiveness and cutting response time.

DOUBTThe source's own "Total Protected Areas" row (1,134 sites, 87,162.91 km², 5.69%) does not arithmetically sum from its own individual-category areas (which add to ≈1,87,162.91 km²) — almost certainly a printing/OCR digit-dropping error (a missing leading "1"). Transcribed exactly as printed; not silently corrected. A separate, seemingly older figure elsewhere in the same source ("903 protected areas... 5.02%") also doesn't match this table — likely simply an earlier snapshot superseded by the February-2025 count used here, but not reconciled with confidence.

6Recent conservation schemes & initiatives (2023–2025)

  • Aravalli Green Wall Project ★ (launched 2023 by the Union Ministry of Environment): a green belt 1,400 km long, 5 km wide, developed in the buffer area around the Aravalli range across 29 districts of four states — Rajasthan, Haryana, Gujarat and Delhi (NCT). Goal: check desertification in North-West India, restore degraded/wasteland forest, increase forest cover, strengthen ecological balance. Focus is afforestation/biodiversity/green-belt creation — not building eco-sensitive highways.
  • Green Credit Programme (GCP) ★: a market-based mechanism under the Environment (Protection) Act, 1986, that rewards individuals/communities/private entities for voluntary environmental actions (afforestation, water conservation) — not a scheme to penalise polluters or a green-bonds vehicle.
  • India's new Eco-Tourism Policy for protected areas ★: aims to balance ecological conservation with local livelihoods via "low-impact tourism," creating employment without damaging the environment.
  • National Aquatic Ecosystem Restoration Mission ★, under the National Mission for Clean Ganga, was expanded in 2025 to include the Yamuna River alongside the Ganga mainstream, for ecological rejuvenation/water-quality improvement/biodiversity conservation.
  • Majhi Vasundhara Campaign 6.0 ★: launched by Maharashtra (announced June 2025), running 1 April 2025–31 March 2026 across 28,317 urban/rural local bodies — a holistic environmental-conservation campaign based on the Panchamahabhuta principle (Earth, Water, Air, Energy/Fire, Space).
HOOKThe Aravalli Green Wall Project is this bucket's single most-repeated fact — tested across at least five near-duplicate PYQs (district/state count, buffer width, objective, states covered, and a statement-pair check) — worth over-learning the core numbers: 5 km wide, 29 districts, 4 states (Rajasthan/Haryana/Gujarat/Delhi), launched 2023.

7Other current-affairs & miscellaneous facts

  • Sundarbans ★: world's largest mangrove forest/delta, shared ≈40% India (West Bengal)/≈60% Bangladesh, formed at the Ganga-Brahmaputra-Meghna confluence; declared a UNESCO World Heritage Site in 1987 for its mangrove ecosystem & biodiversity, and the primary habitat of the Royal Bengal Tiger.
  • Keibul Lamjao National Park ★ (Manipur, part of Loktak Lake) — the world's only floating national park; its floating vegetation islands are called Phumdis.
  • Forest vegetation type ★: coniferous trees/shrubs (needle-leaved, cold-climate adapted) at mid-altitude (1,500–3,000 m) in the Himalayan region are classed as Temperate Forests (Deodar, Oak, Pine) — the tested "wind type of vegetation" question maps to this category, distinct from Tropical Evergreen, Tundra or Desert vegetation.
  • State of India's Environment Report 2024 ★: the Energy sector contributes ≈75% of India's total GHG emissions (the largest single contributor); India's per-capita emissions remain less than half the global average even though its total emissions are high; India ranks very low (176th) on the Environmental Performance Index (EPI) 2024 — so only the "Energy sector is the top contributor" statement is correct among the three checked.
  • Census 2011 ★: Puducherry is the Union Territory with no Scheduled Tribe (ST) community recorded — along with Punjab, Haryana, Chandigarh and Delhi, among the other states/UTs with no recorded ST population.
  • Jhajjar-Bachauli Wildlife Sanctuary ★ (Shivalik Hills, Punjab) is planned to become the state's first leopard-safari destination — promoting leopard conservation, eco-tourism, and public awareness.
  • Typhloperipatus williamsoni ("Velvet Worm") ★ — rediscovered after 111 years in the Siang district of Arunachal Pradesh.
HOOKNearly every fact in this section is a 2024-2025 current-affairs item found only in the PYQ book, not in either Parmar or Lucent — this whole batch leans far more "current affairs/environment GK" than the classical physical-geography content of most earlier batches, reflecting the PYQ book's own bucket contents rather than a build choice.

21 · Global Warming And Climate Change

SSC CGL Tier I · Geography · keyword revision notes · Climate Change causes → Greenhouse Gases & Effect → Global Warming effects → Ozone Depletion → International Agreements & Indian Environmental Law → Air Quality Index → Current climate initiatives & institutes · appendix at end

Last-minute 12

  1. Air Quality Index (AQI) value above 400 (401-500) = the 'Severe' category — causes respiratory effects even in healthy individuals ★
  2. PM2.5 is more harmful than PM10 — its smaller particle size lets it penetrate deep into the lungs and enter the bloodstream ★
  3. Ozone (O₃) is a secondary pollutant in AQI calculations (formed from NOx+VOCs in sunlight) — SO₂, NO₂ and PM are primary pollutants ★
  4. The Central Pollution Control Board (CPCB), 1974, is India's body for monitoring & regulating AQI, via the National Air Quality Monitoring Programme (NAMP) ★
  5. A weakening/collapse of the AMOC (Atlantic Meridional Overturning Circulation) causes cooling in the North Atlantic/Europe and sea-level rise on the US East Coast — NOT increased Arctic warming (the odd one out) ★
  6. The Wadia Institute of Himalayan Geology (WIHG), Dehradun, studies Himalayan glaciers (e.g. Pensilungpa Glacier, Zanskar) and seismology/mountain structures ★
  7. The UNFCCC was adopted in 1992 at the Rio Earth Summit. India's updated NDCs: cut emissions intensity of GDP by 45% by 2030; reach 50% non-fossil-fuel electric capacity by 2030; Net-Zero target is 2070, not 2035 ★
  8. Green Credit Programme (under the Environment (Protection) Act, 1986): rewards verified voluntary actions (afforestation, water harvesting) with tradable credits recognized by financial institutions ★
  9. Chhattisgarh = first Indian state to link its forest ecosystem with Green GDP (adjusting GSDP for the value of natural resources) ★
  10. The single biggest driver of the increasing greenhouse effect today is rising CO₂ concentration — not SO₂, the ozone hole, or CFCs alone ★
  11. Urban Heat Island (UHI): caused by concrete structures, low vegetation and vehicular heat — cities can run 3°C-10°C hotter than surrounding rural areas ★
  12. TRAP ★: building sea walls / elevating buildings against rising seas = Climate Change Adaptation (adjusting to effects already happening) — NOT Mitigation (which means reducing emissions at the source)

1Climate Change: basics & causes

  • Weather = atmospheric conditions of a place at a given time, short-term, not generalizable (studied as Meteorology). Climate = average weather over seasons, generalizable (e.g. "India has a warm climate"). Elements of climate: light, temperature, pressure, wind, humidity/precipitation.
  • Climate Change = long-term shifts in temperature/weather patterns — natural, but human activity has been the main driver since the 1800s, worsening since the 1900s.
  • Causes: Anthropogenic (man-made, now considered dominant) — burning fossil fuels/CO₂ emission, CH₄, CFCs, N₂O, land-use change (deforestation, desertification, urbanisation). Natural — changes in solar intensity, volcanic eruptions, ocean-current shifts.
HOOKHimalayan glaciers are warming/melting faster than other regions due to rising greenhouse-gas concentrations from industrial activity and deforestation — the direct cause tested, distinct from earthquakes or human settlement.

2Greenhouse Gases & the Greenhouse Effect

  • Greenhouse Effect: the atmosphere is transparent to incoming visible sunlight but absorbs outgoing heat radiation from the ground, warming the atmosphere — term coined by French scientist Joseph Fourier. Without it, Earth's average surface temperature would be ≈−18°C to −20°C instead of the actual ≈15°C.
  • Key gases & trends (pre-industrial → recent): CO₂ (most abundant GHG; 280 ppm → ≈368 ppm by 2000; persists 5-200 years) · CH₄ (700 ppb → 1,750 ppb; from wetlands, cattle, rice fields; persists ≈12 years) · CFCs (0 → ≈282 ppt by 2000; from ACs/refrigerants/aerosols; persist 45-260+ years) · N₂O (≈270 ppb → ≈316 ppb; from agriculture, fertilisers, biomass burning).
  • Contribution to global warming: CO₂ ≈60% · CH₄ ≈20% · CFCs ≈14% · N₂O ≈6%. Of CO₂'s share, fossil-fuel combustion contributes ≈75% and deforestation/biomass burning ≈25%.
PYQ ★: "What is mainly responsible for the increase in the greenhouse effect?" → increasing CO₂ concentration — not rising SO₂, the ozone hole, or CFCs alone (CFCs matter more for ozone depletion specifically, covered in §4).

3Global Warming & its effects

  • Global Warming: the gradual rise in Earth's average atmospheric temperature from greenhouse-gas accumulation — term popularised by British environmentalist Wallace Smith Broecker (1970).
  • Effects: Sea-level rise (1-2 mm/yr in the 20th century; up to 0.88 m by 2100 vs. 1990 — from thermal expansion + melting glaciers/ice; low-lying islands like Tuvalu/Kiribati and India's own Ghoramara Island (Sundarbans) already losing land). Severe weather (temperature rise 1.4-5.8°C by 2100; more frequent droughts/floods). Distribution shifts (species migrating poleward/to higher elevations). Declining food production (e.g. SE Asian rice yield falls ≈5% per 1°C rise).
  • Approaches to deal with Global Warming: cut fossil-fuel use & adopt renewables; increase forest cover (CO₂ uptake); develop CFC substitutes; reduce nitrogen-fertiliser use (cuts N₂O).
HOOKCarbon Sink Enhancement ★ in India's climate plan means storing CO₂ in vegetation/forests/soil — the opposite of promoting deforestation or monocultures.

4Ozone Depletion

  • The Ozone layer sits mainly at 20-26 km (some sources: 15-50 km) in the Stratosphere, formed by a dynamic UV-driven photodissociation/recombination equilibrium of O₃ ⇌ O₂+O — this shields Earth's biota from harmful UV.
  • Ozone Hole: spring-time ozone thickness above Antarctica fell from ≈280-325 Dobson Units (1956-1970) to 225 DU (1979), 136 DU (1985), 94 DU (1994) — first discovered 1985 over Antarctica, later confirmed over the Arctic (1990).
  • Cause: CFCs (and to a lesser extent CH₄, N₂O) release chlorine radicals in the stratosphere that catalytically destroy ozone. Sherwood Rowland, Mario Molina and Paul Crutzen won the 1995 Nobel Prize in Chemistry for this discovery.
  • UV types: UV-A (mostly passes through) · UV-B (mostly absorbed by ozone; damages DNA/skin/eyes when it gets through — the main health-harm ray) · UV-C (100% absorbed by an intact ozone layer).
  • Montreal Protocol (Montreal, Canada, 1987; effective 1989) — international treaty to phase out ozone-depleting substances. World Ozone Day, 16 September, commemorates its signing (proclaimed by the UN in 1994).

5International Agreements & Indian Environmental Law

  • International climate agreements: Earth Summit/UNCED (Rio de Janeiro, 1992 — produced the Convention on Biological Diversity and Agenda 21) · Kyoto Protocol (Kyoto, Japan, 1997) · Cancun Agreement (Cancun, Mexico, 2010) · Paris Agreement (Paris, 2015).
  • PYQ ★: "Which treaty was adopted in 1992 to deal with global warming?" → the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) — adopted at the same 1992 Rio Earth Summit.
  • Indian Constitution & environment: Article 21 (Right to Life, includes right to a healthy environment) · Article 48A (State's duty to protect environment/forests/wildlife) · Article 51A(g) (citizens' duty to protect the environment) — all added via the 42nd Amendment, 1976.
  • Key Indian environmental bodies/laws: Wildlife Protection Act 1972 · Water Act 1974 · CPCB, 1974 (technical arm under the Environment (Protection) Act, 1986) · Forest Conservation Act 1980 · Air Act 1981 · Environment (Protection) Act 1986 · Eco-mark, 1991 (BIS certification, symbol = earthen pot) · Biological Diversity Act 2002 · National Green Tribunal (NGT), Delhi, 2010 (Polluter Pays + sustainable development principles).
HOOKUNFCCC, IPCC, explicit COP-numbering (COP26 etc.), and the CBD/CITES acronyms are all genuinely absent from Lucent's own treatment (confirmed, not a missed page) — every PYQ in this bucket that names these is PYQ-book-only content, documented transparently rather than force-fit onto the source books.

6Air Quality Index (AQI)

Pollutant typeExamples
Primary pollutantsSO₂, NO₂, PM10, PM2.5 ★ — emitted directly
Secondary pollutantO₃ (Ozone) ★ — formed from NOx + VOCs in sunlight
  • AQI covers 8 gaseous/particulate pollutants: PM10, PM2.5, NO₂, SO₂, CO, O₃, NH₃, Pb ★ — Noise is NOT included (it's noise pollution, a separate category).
  • AQI >400 (401-500) = 'Severe' category ★ — causes respiratory effects even in healthy people.
  • PM2.5 vs PM10 ★: PM2.5 is more harmful — its smaller size lets it penetrate deep into lungs/bloodstream, raising heart/respiratory disease risk.
  • CPCB (statutory body under the Ministry of Environment, Forest & Climate Change) is primarily responsible for monitoring/regulating AQI in India, via the National Air Quality Monitoring Programme (NAMP) ★.
  • The WHO Ambient Air Quality Database primarily tracks PM10 and PM2.5 concentrations ★ — PM2.5 considered the most lethal.
  • PYQ ★: Assertion-Reason — "Delhi frequently records the highest AQI among Indian cities" (TRUE) / "because it lies in a low-lying basin surrounded by the Himalayas" (FALSE — Delhi's actual geography is between the Ganga-Yamuna plains and the northern Aravallis/Delhi Ridge; its high AQI is really driven by vehicular emissions, construction, and neighbouring-state stubble burning in winter).

7Current climate initiatives & institutes

  • NAFCC (National Adaptation Fund for Climate Change) ★: managed by the Ministry of Environment, Forest & Climate Change; finances climate-adaptation projects proposed by States/UTs (NOT direct payouts to individual farmers). Its Climate Resilient Watershed Programme ★ builds micro-climate resilience via community-led water harvesting in semi-arid, drought-prone zones.
  • LiFE (Lifestyle for Environment) ★: launched by PM Modi at COP26 (Glasgow); promotes sustainable habits — "Reduce, Reuse, Recycle" over a "use-and-throw" mindset — emphasising citizen participation (P3: Pro-Planet People), not government regulation.
  • Carbon markets in India ★: carbon credits generated via afforestation/renewable-energy projects; India launched a domestic voluntary carbon market under the Energy Conservation (Amendment) Act, 2022 — regulated by the Bureau of Energy Efficiency (BEE) (Ministry of Power), NOT the Income Tax Act.
  • Urban Heat Island (UHI) ★: cities run 3°C-10°C hotter than surrounding rural areas due to concrete structures, low vegetation and vehicular heat.
  • Satellite imagery (ISRO's INSAT, RISAT, Resourcesat) ★ gives real-time flood/cyclone/drought data for disaster management — mapping flood extent, tracking cyclones, monitoring drought/landslide risk.
  • Indian Railways ★ cuts carbon emissions mainly by electrifying broad-gauge lines and adopting renewables (solar/wind) at stations — reducing diesel dependence.
  • Increasing urban light pollution most severely hampers observation of deep-sky objects ★ (faint galaxies/nebulae) — bright sunspots, planetary transits and lunar phases are far less affected.
  • National Framework for Climate Services (NFCS), launched 2024 ★: delivers sector-specific climate information (crop forecasts for farmers, cyclone warnings for coasts, heat-wave alerts for health) to agriculture, water, health and disaster-management sectors.

Main objective of the National Framework for Climate Services (2024)SSC CGL TIER-I, 12/09/2025 (Shift-01)Sector-specific climate information services

22 · Wildlife Conservation

SSC CGL Tier I · Geography · keyword revision notes · Biodiversity & the IUCN Red List → Threatened Species of India → Wildlife Protection Act & National Parks → Tiger Reserves & big-mammal conservation → Biosphere Reserves → Named species & sanctuaries → Movements & current initiatives · appendix at end

Last-minute 12

  1. IUCN Red List (2012 revision): 9 categories — Extinct (EX), Extinct in the Wild (EW), Critically Endangered (CR), Endangered (EN), Vulnerable (VU), Near Threatened (NT), Least Concern (LC), Data Deficient (DD), Not Evaluated (NE) — CR+EN+VU together make up "Threatened" species ★
  2. Great Indian Bustard (GIB): Critically Endangered (IUCN); found in the grasslands of Gujarat and Rajasthan (Desert National Park); protected under Schedule I (highest legal protection) of the Wildlife (Protection) Act, 1972 ★
  3. GIB's National Breeding Centre (India's first successful GIB artificial insemination) and the Sudasari GIB Breeding Centre are both in the Desert National Park, Jaisalmer, Rajasthan ★
  4. International Big Cat Alliance (IBCA): India-led, launched 9 April 2023 in Mysuru (marking Project Tiger's 50th anniversary) — covers 7 big cats (Tiger/Lion/Leopard/Snow Leopard/Jaguar/Cheetah/Puma); India's nodal body is the National Tiger Conservation Authority ★
  5. Jim Corbett National Park (Uttarakhand) has India's/the world's highest tiger count in any reserve (>150, per 2024 data) — and was the first National Park to implement Project Tiger (1973) ★
  6. Kaziranga (Assam) = India's largest One-horned Rhinoceros habitat, with 148 tigers per the "Status of Tigers in Kaziranga, 2024" report; Jaldapara (West Bengal) = the 2nd-largest rhino habitat ★
  7. National Parks under the Wildlife Protection Act, 1972: exist to protect wildlife & their habitats — hunting, felling and grazing are banned inside them; NOT for tourism, trade regulation, or elections ★
  8. Bandipur (Karnataka, part of the Nilgiri Biosphere Reserve) and Amrabad (Telangana, carved from Nagarjunasagar-Srisailam Tiger Reserve, in the Nallamala Hills) are two frequently-tested Tiger Reserves ★
  9. Nicobar Megapode = a bird endemic only to Great Nicobar Island, found nowhere else. Fishing Cat tracking via radio-collar first launched at Coringa Wildlife Sanctuary (Andhra Pradesh); Fishing Cats live in coastal wetlands/mangroves ★
  10. Bhitarkanika (Odisha) = India's 2nd-largest mangrove ecosystem after the Sundarbans, famous for saltwater crocodiles ★
  11. Keibul Lamjao National Park (Manipur, on Loktak Lake) = the world's only floating national park — floating islands called Phumdis ★
  12. Narmada Bachao Andolan (1985), led by Medha Patkar, protested large dam construction on the Narmada river ★

1Biodiversity & the IUCN Red List

  • Biodiversity = variety of life within a region (Greek bios + Latin diversitas). Term coined by Walter G. Rosen (1985), popularised by E.O. Wilson ("Father of Biodiversity"). India: only 2.4% of world land area but 8.1% of global species diversity — one of 12 mega-diversity countries. Most biodiversity-rich Indian zones: North-East (5.2%) and Western Ghats (4%) of geographical area.
  • Loss of biodiversity — "The Evil Quartet": habitat loss/fragmentation, introduction of exotic/alien species, over-exploitation, co-extinctions.
  • Baseline records: Red Data Book (IUCN, started 1963) · Blue Book (UNEP, endangered species worldwide) · Green Book (Botanical Survey of India, rare plants in protected gardens).
CategoryMeaning
Extinct (EX)No reasonable doubt the last individual has died
Extinct in the Wild (EW)Survives only in captivity/cultivation
Critically Endangered (CR)Extremely high extinction risk, immediate future
Endangered (EN)Very high risk, near future
Vulnerable (VU)High risk, medium-term future
Near Threatened / Least Concern / Data Deficient / Not EvaluatedProgressively lower concern or insufficient data
HOOKIUCN Red List = called "Life's Barometer." CR+EN+VU together are what's meant by "Threatened" species — the GIB (§2) and several species in §6 fall in this cluster.

2Threatened Species of India

CategoryExamples
Critically EndangeredPygmy Hog (Manas NP), Vulture (Gyps indicus), Sumatran Rhino, Namdapha Flying Squirrel
EndangeredRed Panda, Asiatic Wild Dog/Dhole, Lion-tailed Macaque, Nilgiri Tahr, Golden Langur
VulnerableBlack Buck, Greater One-horned Rhino, Sloth Bear, Cheetah (historically), Dugong, Indian Gaur
CASE STUDYVultures: population crashed 97-99% within a decade (from ≈80 million in India+Pakistan, early 1990s) — cause: kidney failure from the cattle painkiller diclofenac in carcasses vultures fed on. Consequence: exploding feral-dog population (also scavenging carcasses) spread rabies, giving India the world's highest human rabies death toll. Banned for veterinary use in 2006 (India/Pakistan/Nepal); 9 vulture conservation centres now operate in India.

3Wildlife Protection Act, 1972 & National Parks

  • Wildlife (Protection) Act, 1972: India's principal wildlife law, grants species Schedule-based legal protection — Schedule I species get the highest protection (e.g. GIB, Tiger).
  • PYQ ★: role of National Parks under this Act → protect wildlife and their natural habitats — hunting, tree-felling and grazing are all banned inside them (not tourism promotion, trade regulation, or elections).
HOOKJim Corbett National Park (originally Hailey NP, 1936 — cross-refer Batch 20) was the first National Park to implement Project Tiger (1973), and today also holds the single highest tiger count of any reserve worldwide (>150, 2024 data).

4Tiger Reserves & big-mammal conservation

  • International Big Cat Alliance (IBCA) ★: India-initiated and led; launched 9 April 2023 in Mysuru, Karnataka, marking Project Tiger's 50th anniversary. Promotes international cooperation/scientific data-sharing for 7 big cats — Tiger, Lion, Leopard, Snow Leopard, Cheetah, Jaguar, Puma (of which India itself hosts Tiger/Lion/Leopard/Snow Leopard/Cheetah, excluding Puma and Jaguar). Nodal Indian body: National Tiger Conservation Authority.
  • Bandipur Tiger Reserve ★ — Karnataka, part of the Nilgiri Biosphere Reserve, one of India's oldest reserves (tiger/elephant/leopard/gaur habitat).
  • Amrabad Tiger Reserve ★ — Telangana; carved out of the Nagarjunasagar-Srisailam Tiger Reserve after state reorganisation; in the Nallamala Hills, Krishna River flows through it.
  • Kaziranga (Assam) ★ = India's largest One-horned Rhinoceros habitat; Jaldapara (West Bengal) = the 2nd-largest. Per the "Status of Tigers in Kaziranga, 2024" report, Kaziranga also holds 148 tigers.
  • Karnataka has the highest elephant population among South Indian states (notably Bandipur/Nagarhole) ★ — but also records a high number of juvenile elephant deaths there, caused by Elephant Endotheliotropic Herpesvirus (EEHV).

5Biosphere Reserves — state & feature matching

Biosphere ReserveStateNotable feature
Seshachalam HillsAndhra PradeshFamous for red sandalwood
Dihang-DibangArunachal PradeshMishmi Hills & Dibang Valley
PachmarhiMadhya PradeshKnown as the "Queen of Satpura"
Dibru-SaikhowaAssamAlong the Brahmaputra & Lohit rivers
SimilipalOdishaTiger Reserve + UNESCO Biosphere Reserve (cross-refer Batch 20)
PYQ ★: correctly-matched Biosphere Reserve/state pairs — Seshachalam Hills-Andhra Pradesh is correct; Dehang-Dibang is in Arunachal Pradesh (NOT Meghalaya), Pachmarhi is in Madhya Pradesh (NOT Gujarat), Dibru-Saikhowa is in Assam (NOT Manipur) — the classic "which pair is right" trap tests exactly these four.
PYQ ★: National Park/State pairs — Similipal National Park-Odisha is correct; Namdapha is in Arunachal Pradesh (not Nagaland), Bandipur is in Karnataka (not Andhra Pradesh), Vansda National Park is in Gujarat (not Rajasthan).

6Named species & sanctuaries

  • Nicobar Megapode ★ — a bird endemic only to Great Nicobar Island (found naturally nowhere else in the world) — not the Lion-tailed Macaque, Indian Pangolin, or Great Hornbill.
  • Fishing Cat ★ — India's first radio-collar tracking project launched at Coringa Wildlife Sanctuary (Andhra Pradesh); the species lives in coastal wetlands and mangrove ecosystems.
  • The endangered black-collared yellow catfish ★ was recently in the news from the Chalakudy River (Western Ghats, Kerala).
  • Bhitarkanika Mangroves ★ — Odisha; India's 2nd-largest mangrove ecosystem after the Sundarbans, famous for saltwater crocodiles.
  • Salkhan Fossil Park ★ — Sonbhadra district, Uttar Pradesh; famous for stromatolite fossils (≈1.4 billion years old, among the earliest evidence of life on Earth).
  • Umred-Pauni-Karhandla Wildlife Sanctuary ★ — Maharashtra (near Nagpur); a tiger-conservation corridor linked to the Tadoba-Andhari Tiger Reserve.
  • Parvati Arga Bird Sanctuary ★ — Gonda district, Uttar Pradesh; a Ramsar wetland site (two lakes, Parvati & Arga) hosting migratory birds from Central Asia/Tibet — cross-refer Batch 20's Ramsar Sites section.
  • Sudasari GIB Breeding Centre ★ — Desert National Park, Jaisalmer, Rajasthan — for Godawan (Great Indian Bustard) conservation/breeding.
  • Keibul Lamjao National Park ★ — Manipur (Loktak Lake) — the world's only floating national park (cross-refer Batch 20).
  • Forest/Bengal Owl ★ — endemic to Central/Eastern India, listed Endangered on the IUCN Red List since 2018; threatened by habitat loss, hunting and climate change.

7Conservation movements & other current initiatives

  • Narmada Bachao Andolan ★ (1985) — led by Medha Patkar (with Baba Amte and others), a social movement protesting large dams on the Narmada river, spanning Gujarat/Maharashtra/MP — one of India's largest environment-and-human-rights movements (cross-refer the fuller Environmental Movements table reserved from Batch 21).
HOOKIndia's first e-waste recycling park ★ is being built in Delhi (≈20 acres) for scientific disposal/recycling of old mobiles, laptops and other electronics — a current-affairs fact that appears in this bucket despite not being a "wildlife" topic itself, most likely bundled in as general environment/conservation current affairs.
