23 · Ecosystem

SSC CGL Tier I · Geography · keyword revision notes · Ecology basics & hierarchy → Ecological factors → Ecosystem types & components → Food chain, trophic levels & the 10% Law → Biosphere & biogeochemical cycles → Current ecosystem/conservation topics · appendix at end

Last-minute 12

  1. Ecology (Greek Oikos=house/habitat + Logos=study): usually credited to Ernst Haeckel (1869), though H. Reiter used the term first in 1868. E.P. Odum = father of Modern Ecology; Ramdeo Misra = father of Indian Ecology ★
  2. Ecological Hierarchy (7 levels): Organism → Population → Community → Ecosystem → Landscape → Biome → Biosphere — mnemonic "Only Prayer Can Enable Life Beautiful, Blissful"
  3. Term "Ecosystem" coined by Arthur Tansley (1935); the Sun is the ecosystem's ultimate driving force (energy flows one-way, matter cycles)
  4. Ecosystem Components: Abiotic (inorganic/organic materials, climatic elements) + Biotic — Producers, and 4 Consumer categories (Primary/Herbivore, Secondary/Carnivore, Tertiary/top-carnivore, Quaternary/Decomposers) ★
  5. Decomposers (fungi/bacteria) break down dead plants and animals, releasing nutrients back into the ecosystem — without them, nutrients stay locked in dead matter and biosphere life would stop ★
  6. Ecological Pyramid (energy, kcal): 1st/Producer=1000 → 2nd/Herbivore=100 → 3rd/Carnivore=10 → 4th/Top-carnivore=1 — only ≈10% of energy passes up each level (Lindeman's 10% Law, Raymond Lindeman, 1942)
  7. Carrying capacity = the maximum population size an environment can sustain indefinitely without degrading it ★
  8. Rivet Popper Hypothesis (Paul Ehrlich): species in an ecosystem are like rivets on an aircraft — losing a few may not be catastrophic, but each loss raises the risk of collapse; used to argue every species (not just "key" ones) matters to ecosystem stability ★
  9. Francis Evans (1956) referred to the ecosystem as the "basic unit in ecology" ★
  10. Carbon Cycle: Photosynthesis + Respiration + Combustion = the Carbon Cycle; CO2 is ≈0.03% of the atmosphere; excess CO2 from fossil-fuel combustion is the direct driver of higher temperatures/global warming (cross-refer Batch 21)
  11. Biosphere: average thickness ≈30 km; most life concentrated between 500 m below the ocean surface and 6,000 m above sea level; NASA has detected dormant bacterial spores up to 8,000 m and living bacteria in air up to 15 km
  12. Solar energy is the primary energy source driving the Water Cycle ★; among trophic levels, Producers (plants) suffer the most immediate/severe biomass impact from prolonged drought, since photosynthesis directly depends on water availability ★

Key

TRAPeasily confused pair
PYQa real past SSC paper tested this — exact citation given
HOOKmemory aid — added by Claude, not from sources
DOUBTsources conflict / unverified
★ tested in geography_pyq.pdf (exam+date+shift in the appendix) · no personal attempt data exists for this source, so there are no "my mistake" boxes anywhere in this build

1Ecology — basics, coinage & hierarchy

  • Ecology: Greek Oikos (house/habitat) + Logos (study) — the scientific study of interactions between organisms and their environment. Coinage disputed: H. Reiter used it first (1868), but it is more widely credited to Ernst Haeckel (1869). E.P. Odum's Fundamentals of Ecology quotes Haeckel's own definition of the term.
  • "Father" attributions: Ernst Haeckel = father of Ecology (Classical period) — alternatively Alexander Von Humboldt; E.P. Odum = father of Modern Ecology; Ramdeo Misra = father of Indian Ecology.
  • Ecology's historical progression: Classical period → Modern period → "Global Ecology" (roughly 1975-2000), reflecting the shift to planet-scale environmental concerns.
LevelDefinition (brief)
OrganismA single living individual (note: a virus is not considered an organism)
PopulationGroup of the same species in one area
CommunityAll populations of different species interacting in an area
EcosystemCommunity + its abiotic environment, functioning together
LandscapeA cluster of interacting ecosystems
BiomeLarge regional/global ecosystem type (e.g. desert, grassland)
Biosphere / EcosphereAll of Earth's ecosystems combined — the "life zone"
HOOKEcology's 7-level hierarchy is a condensed version of Biology's fuller 11-level hierarchy (which adds Cell/Tissue/Organ/Organ-system below Organism). Mnemonic: "Only Prayer Can Enable Life Beautiful, Blissful" = Organism-Population-Community-Ecosystem-Landscape-Biome-Biosphere.

2Ecological factors — abiotic & biotic

  • Branches of Ecology: by scope — Autecology (single species/individual) vs. Synecology (communities); also classified by taxonomic affinity, and by habitat — Terrestrial vs. Aquatic Ecology.
  • Abiotic factors: Light (photoperiod; plants classed as photophilous/sun-loving or sciophilous/shade-loving), Temperature (species classed Megatherms/Mesotherms/Microtherms/Hekistotherms by heat tolerance), Humidity, Wind (graded Breeze→Gale→Storm→Hurricane by speed), Topographic factors, and Edaphic/Soil factors (soil composition).
  • Biotic factors (inter-species relationships): Symbiosis (mutual benefit, e.g. lichen), Parasitism (one benefits, one harmed), Commensalism (one benefits, other unaffected), Predation (one kills/eats another), Saprophytism (feeding on dead/decaying matter).
  • Ecology glossary: Deep Ecology/Ecosophy (Arne Naess, 1972 — nature has intrinsic worth beyond human use); Ecotone (transition zone between two ecosystems, e.g. mangrove); Ecological Footprint (humanity's demand is >1.5 global hectares/person — roughly 50% more than Earth can sustainably regenerate); Niche (Joseph Grinnell, 1917 — a species' functional role/position); Overshoot (demand exceeding an ecosystem's regenerative capacity).
  • Ecological Succession (Frederic Clements): the gradual, directional change of a community over time — stages Nudation → Invasion → Ecesis → Competition → Reaction → Stabilisation; Primary succession (bare, lifeless substrate) vs. Secondary succession (disturbed but not lifeless, e.g. after fire).
  • Ecological Transition (John W. Bennatt, 1976) sic — societal shift in how humans relate to/exploit their environment over time (the source book prints the surname "Bennatt"; the concept is usually attributed in other literature to anthropologist John W. Bennett — see A.3).

3Ecosystem — types & components

  • Term "Ecosystem" coined by Arthur Tansley (1935). The Sun is the ultimate driving force of every ecosystem.
  • Types: Natural/Non-cultivated — Terrestrial (forest, grassland, desert) and Aquatic (marine, lake, pond); Artificial/Cultivated — crop field, park, spacecraft, swimming pool, well, aquarium.
  • Abiotic components: Inorganic materials, Organic materials, and Climatic elements — together forming the ecosystem's relatively constant "standing state."
Biotic componentRole & examples
ProducersConvert solar energy via photosynthesis (plants; phytoplankton = ocean's primary producers); only ≈1-10% of absorbed solar energy is converted to usable chemical energy
Primary consumersHerbivores — feed directly on producers
Secondary consumersCarnivores (feed on herbivores) — includes Omnivores (feed on both plants & animals)
Tertiary consumersTop carnivores — feed on secondary consumers
Quaternary consumers / DecomposersSaprotrophs/Detritivores (fungi, bacteria) — break down dead organic matter ★
HOOKWithout Decomposers, nutrients would stay permanently locked inside dead organisms and never re-enter the soil/water — effectively halting all life in the biosphere. This is exactly the PYQ-tested definition (§ term for organisms that break down dead plants/animals).

4Food chain, trophic levels & the 10% Law

  • Food Chain: the sequential transfer of energy from producers through successive consumer levels. Simple example: Grass → Grasshopper → Snake → Hawk. A network of interconnected food chains = Food Web (Complex food chain).
  • Undigested food enters the detritus path, feeding decomposers rather than the next trophic level directly.
  • Chemosynthesis: deep-sea bacteria near hydrothermal vents produce food using geothermal/chemical energy instead of sunlight — an exception to the Sun-driven rule.
Trophic levelEnergy (kcal, illustrative)
1st — Producers1000
2nd — Herbivores100
3rd — Carnivores10
4th — Top carnivores1
TRAP ★ Prolonged drought's most immediate/severe biomass impact falls on Producers (not herbivores/carnivores/decomposers) — plants depend directly on water for photosynthesis, so they're first and hardest hit; the effect only cascades upward afterward.
HOOKLindeman's 10% Law (Raymond Lindeman, 1942): only ≈10% of energy at one trophic level transfers to the next — the rest is lost as heat/respiration. Classic example: ≈100 kg of grain is needed to produce just ≈10 kg of meat. This is why food chains rarely exceed 4-5 levels.

5Biosphere & biogeochemical cycles

  • Biosphere: Greek etymology; Joy Tivy defined it as the "organic world." Average thickness ≈30 km. Most life is concentrated between 500 m below the ocean surface and 6,000 m above sea level — largely absent at the poles, on the highest mountains, and in the deepest oceans. NASA has detected dormant bacterial spores up to 8,000 m altitude and living bacteria in air up to 15 km.
  • The Biosphere is not a separate/4th sphere — it is the shared, life-supporting overlap of the lithosphere, hydrosphere and atmosphere. Known species: >3 lakh plant species, >10 lakh animal species.
  • Water Cycle: driven primarily by Solar energy ★ — evaporation from oceans/water bodies, condensation, precipitation, and return flow, all powered by the Sun's heat.
  • Bio-geo-chemical Cycle: the continuous movement of nutrients/matter between living organisms and the non-living environment. Biogeochemistry as a discipline was founded by Vladimir Ivanovich Vernadsky (1926). Key cycles: Water, Carbon, Nitrogen, Oxygen, Phosphorous.
  • Carbon Cycle: Photosynthesis (fixes atmospheric CO2 into plants) → Consumers → Decomposers → Soils/fossil deposits (Coal/Oil) → Combustion → back to atmospheric CO2, plus the direct Respiration return path. Formula: Photosynthesis + Respiration + Combustion = Carbon Cycle. CO2 makes up only ≈0.03% of the atmosphere by volume, yet excess CO2 from fossil-fuel burning is explicitly linked (this is the one direct Global-Warming linkage in this section, distinct from Batch 21's dedicated climate-change treatment) to higher temperatures, global warming and climate change.
  • Energy vs. matter movement: energy flows uni-directionally (Sun→Producers→Herbivores→Carnivores→Decomposers, with ≈90% dissipated as heat at each step per Lindeman's Law), while matter/nutrients cycle repeatedly — Earth is a closed system for matter (Law of Conservation of Energy applies), so nutrients are reused, never created or destroyed.

6Current ecosystem & conservation topics

  • Rivet Popper Hypothesis ★ (Paul Ehrlich): compares species in an ecosystem to rivets on an aircraft wing — removing a few rivets may not immediately cause failure, but each removal raises collapse risk; argues that even seemingly "minor" species contribute to overall ecosystem stability and that species richness itself matters for ecosystem maintenance.
  • Human-induced disturbances mainly impact terrestrial ecosystems through changes in land use ★ (e.g. deforestation, urbanisation, agricultural conversion) rather than through any single other factor.
  • Management of subsurface fluid reservoirs (groundwater/oil/gas) is important for both economic and environmental reasons ★ — sustainable extraction avoids resource depletion while preventing land subsidence and ecological damage.
  • Chillai Kalan ★: the local name in Jammu and Kashmir (Kashmir Valley) for the region's harshest 40-day winter period, 21 December to ≈30 January.
  • Per "EnviStats India 2024: Environment Accounts" (MoSPI), India's mangrove coverage increased by ≈8% ★ between 2013 and 2021, driven by conservation/afforestation efforts in West Bengal, Odisha and Gujarat.
  • Nilgiri Tahr ★ (Nilgiritragus hylocrius) — State Animal of Tamil Nadu; found in the high-altitude grasslands (1,200-2,600 m) of the Southern Western Ghats (Tamil Nadu/Kerala); IUCN status Endangered, subject of ongoing conservation efforts.
  • Coral bleaching in the Indian Ocean ★ is most directly caused by anthropogenically induced increases in sea surface temperature — heat-stressed corals expel their symbiotic algae (Zooxanthellae), losing colour and their main energy source.
  • Shola ecosystem ★: tropical montane forest patches (stunted evergreen "Sholas") growing in valley depressions/folds of the higher Western Ghats (Kerala/Tamil Nadu/Karnataka), surrounded by rolling grasslands — NOT an arid/semi-arid or conifer-dominated system; called "nature's water towers" for their role in sustaining Western Ghats river systems, and noted for high endemic biodiversity.
  • Permaculture ★ relates to the development of sustainable agricultural ecosystems — developed in the 1970s by Bill Mollison and David Holmgren, designed to work with (not against) natural patterns.
  • India's major coral reef ecosystems ★ are concentrated in the Gulf of Kutch, Gulf of Mannar, Lakshadweep, and Andaman & Nicobar Islands.
  • Sarsai Nawar Wetland ★ (Etawah district, Uttar Pradesh) — a man-made, rain-fed wetland; Ramsar Site since 2019; newly recognised for holding the largest Sarus Crane population in India; carries a cultural connection to Maharishi Valmiki. The Sarus Crane is UP's state bird and the world's tallest flying bird.
  • The "Mangrove Cell" initiative (2025) ★ is primarily linked to Maharashtra (Thane/Mumbai/Raigad/Sindhudurg districts, ≈320 sq km of mangrove area) — aims at coastal-wetland conservation, mangrove plantation, and promoting the Blue Economy.
  • Both are correct ★: afforestation can help prevent floods, and afforestation helps balance ecosystems — standard, non-trick statement-pair.

24 · Miscellaneous

SSC CGL Tier I · Geography · keyword revision notes · Population, Settlements & Social Geography → Geological Timescale, Landforms & terms → Environmental Movements → India's Water-Sharing Treaties & Border Geography → World Geography & Strategic Locations → Current Schemes, Missions & Current Affairs · appendix at end

Last-minute 12

  1. Rural settlement types: Compact/Clustered (houses close together), Semi-compact (sub-clustered), Hamleted (scattered hamlets around a main village), Dispersed (widely separated houses — hilly/forested/sparsely-populated regions) ★
  2. Factors affecting population distribution/density: Geographical (water availability, landforms, climate, soil), Economic (minerals, urbanisation, industrialisation), Social & Cultural — fertile plains with good climate/resources/infrastructure = high density (e.g. Ganga Plain) ★
  3. Don't conflate: Bishnoi Movement (1730, Khejarli/Marwar, Rajasthan, led by Amrita Devi — saving khejri trees) is a full two centuries older than the Chipko Movement (started March 1973, Chamoli & Tehri Garhwal, Uttarakhand — Chandi Prasad Bhatt/Sunderlal Bahuguna/Gaura Devi, villagers hugging trees) ★
  4. Geographical Timescale (Eon→Era→Period→Epoch): Precambrian = origin of life (sea) → Paleozoic = first fish/amphibians/reptiles → Mesozoic = dinosaurs' dominance, first mammals/birds → Cenozoic = mammals diversify, human evolution ★
  5. Outwash Plain = sorted, rounded meltwater-stream sediments beyond a glacier (cross-refer Batch 17/Lithosphere) — NOT the angular, unsorted till directly deposited by stagnant ice (that's a Moraine) ★
  6. Watershed/Water Divide = elevated boundary separating the drainage basins of two different river systems — e.g. the Delhi Ridge divides the Ganga and Indus drainage basins ★
  7. Suez Canal connects the Mediterranean Sea to the Red Sea (opened 1869); Chabahar Port (Iran, on the Gulf of Oman) gives India trade access to Afghanistan/Central Asia while bypassing Pakistan ★
  8. Bhutan = "Land of the Thunder Dragon" (Druk Yul) — named for storms/thunder in the Himalayan valleys, believed to be the dragon's voice; a white dragon ("Druk") features on its national flag ★
  9. Chagos Archipelago: disputed between the United Kingdom and Mauritius — UK agreed (late 2024) to transfer sovereignty to Mauritius but retains control of the Diego Garcia military base ★
  10. Great Nicobar = India's largest island by area (≈1,045 km², also India's southernmost point/Indira Point) — do NOT confuse with Majuli (Assam, Brahmaputra) = world's largest river island, which is much smaller in area ★
  11. Don't conflate India's water treaties: Farakka Agreement (12 Dec 1996, India-Bangladesh, Ganga water-sharing at the Farakka Barrage, lean/dry season only — cross-refer Batch 6) vs. Mahakali Treaty (1996, India-Nepal, integrated development of the Mahakali river + Pancheshwar Multipurpose Project) ★
  12. Mission SAGAR (India → Indian Ocean Region, ongoing humanitarian/medical assistance) vs. Operation Brahma (March 2025, India → earthquake-hit Myanmar, medical/food/tarpaulin/hygiene-kit relief) — two different, contemporary Indian relief missions ★

1Population, settlements & social geography

  • Factors influencing population distribution: Geographical (water availability, landforms, climate, soil), Economic (minerals, urbanisation, industrialisation), Social & Cultural. High density results where fertile land, favourable climate, abundant resources and robust economic opportunity coincide — the Ganga Plain is the classic Indian example ★.
  • Rural settlement types: Compact/Clustered — houses built closely together; Semi-compact/Semi-clustered — a cluster divided into sub-clusters; Hamleted — one or more hamlets scattered around/away from the main village; Dispersed — houses widely separated, typically on rugged terrain with limited farmland (Meghalaya, Uttarakhand, Himachal Pradesh, Kerala) ★.
  • Temporary settlements are built in harsh/marginal environments where people stay only briefly for hunting, gathering or transhumance — cold deserts, dense forests, rugged mountain slopes ★.
TRAP ★ Dispersed rural settlements in India are found predominantly in Bundelkhand (central India), Chhattisgarh, Uttarakhand and Himachal Pradesh — hilly/rugged terrain with limited farmland; the Ganga's lower plains, by contrast, favour dense/compact settlement, not dispersed.
HOOK ★ Mineral deposits raise population density indirectly — they attract industries, which create jobs (transport, trade, manufacturing), pulling in workers — not by improving soil fertility or reducing employment need.
HOOK ★ Study-of-life terminology: Palaeontologists study fossils, Geologists study rocks/earth structure, Ornithologists study birds — Anthropologists study human societies and cultures from the oldest times to the present.

2Geological timescale, landforms & geographical terms

EraKey life event
PrecambrianOrigin of life (in the sea)
PaleozoicFirst fish, amphibians, reptiles, vascular plants
MesozoicDinosaurs' dominance; first mammals, first birds, first flowering plants
CenozoicMammals diversify; human evolution
TRAP ★ Match carefully: 1-Precambrian = origin of life, 2-Mesozoic = dinosaurs' dominance, 3-Cenozoic = first mammals diversify/human evolution — a common distractor swaps "first mammals" into the wrong era (first mammals actually first appear in the Triassic, within the Mesozoic, but they only diversify in the Cenozoic).
  • Outwash Plain (Geographical-terms glossary) = an alluvial plain formed by streams originating from a glacier's melting ice — sorted, rounded sediment carried by running meltwater (cross-refer Batch 17/Lithosphere's glacial-landforms section, which built this term's fuller depositional-landforms context) ★.
  • Watershed = an elevated boundary line separating headstreams that are tributaries to different river systems/basins — i.e. a water divide. The Delhi Ridge is the classic Indian example, separating the Ganga and Indus drainage basins ★.
  • Desert (xerophytic) plants — e.g. Cactus, pineapple — use CAM (Crassulacean Acid Metabolism): stomata open at night to take in CO₂ (stored as malic acid) and stay closed during the hot day, conserving water ★.

3Environmental movements — Chipko & Bishnoi

  • Bishnoi Movement — 1730, Khejarli village, Marwar region, Jodhpur district, Rajasthan — led by Amrita Devi (a follower of the Bishnoi faith founded by Guru Jambhoji); villagers (363+ Bishnois, including Amrita Devi and her three daughters) died protecting sacred khejri trees from the Maharaja of Jodhpur's soldiers.
  • Chipko Movement ("Chipko" = hug the tree) — started March 1973, Chamoli & Tehri Garhwal districts, Uttarakhand (then U.P.) — led by Chandi Prasad Bhatt, Sunderlal Bahuguna and Gaura Devi; villagers, especially women, hugged trees to stop forest contractors' axes ★. 2023 marked the movement's 50th anniversary (of its modern form, not its origin).
TRAP ★ The Chipko Movement did NOT originate in the 18th century with the Bishnoi community — that describes the separate, earlier Bishnoi Movement (1730). Chipko itself began in 1973 in Uttarakhand's Himalayan regions; its methods were peaceful (tree-hugging), never violent protests, lawsuits, or arson.
HOOKBoth movements share the theme "protect trees with your own body," which is exactly why PYQs test them against each other — keep the century (1730 vs. 1973) and the state (Rajasthan vs. Uttarakhand) as the two anchors that separate them.

4India's water-sharing treaties & border geography

  • India-Nepal WASH (Water, Sanitation and Hygiene) MoU ★: covers groundwater management, water-quality control, sanitation-system improvement, capacity building and technology sharing — does NOT include "privatized sanitation benchmarks" or real-time glacial-runoff tracking.
  • Jal Shakti Abhiyan ★: aims to improve declining groundwater levels through Rainwater Harvesting, ensuring water availability in water-stressed regions — not to increase groundwater depletion or promote urbanisation.
  • Kosi Agreement (1954) and Gandak Agreement (1959) ★: joint India-Nepal management (construction/maintenance/operation) of barrages for irrigation/flood control (Kosi Agreement cross-refer Batch 6/Rivers of India).
  • Mahakali Treaty (1996) ★: India-Nepal treaty for the integrated development of the Mahakali river's waters, including the Pancheshwar Multipurpose Project (Sharada Barrage, Tanakpur Barrage) — NOT unilateral development by either side.
TRAP ★ Farakka Agreement (1996) = India-Bangladesh, sharing Ganga water at the Farakka Barrage, applies to the lean/dry season only, NOT the entire year (cross-refer Batch 6). Mahakali Treaty (1996) = India-Nepal, a different river, different neighbour, different scope — do not merge the two "1996 river treaties."
DOUBT ★ Per the PYQ book's own explanation: India-Nepal border tensions persist at Kalapani (India considers Kalapani, Nepal considers Lipulekh, the river's actual source) and Susta (encroachment plus boundary confusion from the shifting course of the Narayani/Gandak river) — despite the 1816 Treaty of Sugauli (cross-refer Batch 1) and over 90% of the boundary strip maps being finalised. Genuinely live/evolving border geography, not resolved in either source textbook.

5World geography & strategic locations

  • Bhutan ★ = "Land of the Thunder Dragon" (Druk Yul) — named for the severe Himalayan storms/thunder traditionally believed to be the dragon's voice; a white dragon ("Druk") appears on Bhutan's national flag, symbolising purity and loyalty.
  • Chabahar Port ★ (Sistan-Baluchestan province, Iran, on the Gulf of Oman, developed by India) — primary strategic significance: gives India direct trade access to Afghanistan and Central Asia while bypassing Pakistan; strengthens the INSTC and regional connectivity.
  • Suez Canal ★ connects the Mediterranean Sea to the Red Sea (via the Gulf of Aden) — a major global Indian-Ocean-linked trade route between Europe and Asia; NOT the Panama Canal (Atlantic-Pacific), Bering Strait, or Drake Passage.
  • Chagos Archipelago ★ — Indian Ocean island group; disputed between the United Kingdom (long-time administrator) and Mauritius (sovereignty claimant); UK agreed in late 2024 to transfer sovereignty to Mauritius while retaining control of the Diego Garcia military base.
  • Great Nicobar Island ★ = India's largest island by area (≈1,045 km²), southernmost tip of the Andaman & Nicobar archipelago, home to Indira Point (India's southernmost point) — cross-refer Batch 4 (Plains, Ports and Islands). Not Majuli (world's largest river island, but far smaller by area), Rameswaram, or Sriharikota.

6Current schemes, missions & current affairs

  • Mission SAGAR ★ (Security and Growth for All in the Region) — India's initiative extending medical/food/humanitarian assistance to Indian Ocean Region countries; part of India's "Neighbourhood First" and SAGAR Vision policy.
  • Operation Brahma ★ (March 2025) — India's earthquake-relief mission to Myanmar (medical aid, food, tarpaulins, hygiene kits).
  • Mission Mausam ★ — India is building a cloud chamber (artificially simulating cloud formation, to improve cloud-seeding/artificial-rain techniques) at the Indian Institute of Tropical Meteorology (IITM), Pune.
  • Indian Ocean Rim Association (IORA) ★ — established 1997; promotes economic cooperation, trade, maritime security and sustainable development across the Indian Ocean region.
  • PM Narendra Modi ★ addressed a 2025 special session on "Environment, COP-30, and Global Health" (during the 17th BRICS Summit, Rio de Janeiro, July 2025), emphasising India's climate action and the "One Earth, One Health" approach.
  • Real-time AQI monitoring ★ in urban areas increasingly uses remote sensing/satellite data (optical sensors analysing atmospheric particle/gas concentration) for broader coverage than ground stations — cross-refer Batch 21's AQI mechanics.
  • India-Bangladesh defence cooperation: an MoU to set up 20 coastal surveillance radar stations along Bangladesh's coast, for real-time "Maritime Domain Awareness" in the Bay of Bengal ★.
  • Zero-emission public transport commonly uses Electric buses (battery-run, no tailpipe combustion or emissions) ★.
  • National Logistics Policy ★ launched September 2022 — aims to reduce logistics costs, strengthen the supply chain, improve Ease of Doing Business.
  • Post-Independence land reforms (zamindari abolition, land ceiling, sharecropper rights) were successfully implemented in Kerala and West Bengal ★.
  • SEZ Rules, 2006 ★ came into effect in February 2006 (the parent SEZ Act, 2005, received presidential assent in June 2005).
  • Production of inorganic chemicals (sulphuric acid, soda ash — used in fertilisers/detergents/glass) in India is widespread nationally, not confined to northern states ★.
  • Per the PYQ book's own explanation, the Gaddi tribe's shepherds traditionally lived/grazed in the Western Himalayas in the fourteenth century low-confidence — the PYQ book's printed answer-explanation text for this question is garbled/mismatched (see A.3 DOUBT); this is the standard, independently-known association for the Gaddi community and not confirmed here against the source's own explanation.

A.2 Cross-book & cross-file notes

  • This "Miscellaneous" bucket draws on the two source files that carry no dedicated PYQ sub-topic of their own — `human_geography_raw_extraction.md` (Parmar book pp.84-86 + Lucent book pp.153-154, used here for the first time in this build) and `world_map_raw_extraction.md` (used here for the first time) — plus the Environmental Movements table (book pp.199-200, reserved since Batch 21) and single cross-referenced facts already built into Batches 1, 4, 6, 17 and 21.
  • Outwash Plain (§2) is cross-referenced to Batch 17 (Lithosphere), which built the fuller glacial-depositional-landforms context (Moraines/Eskers/Drumlins) — not rebuilt here.
  • The Farakka Agreement (§4) is cross-referenced to Batch 6 (Rivers of India); Great Nicobar as India's largest island (§5) to Batch 4 (Plains, Ports and Islands); the Treaty of Sugauli/India-Nepal border (§4) to Batch 1 (India and Its Location); AQI remote-sensing (§6) to Batch 21 (Global Warming And Climate Change).
  • Roughly 25 of this bucket's 40 PYQs (§1's social-geography/language-family/anthropologist items, and nearly all of §6) are current-affairs or general-knowledge facts not found in either source book — documented transparently as PYQ-book-only throughout, drawing on the PYQ book's own explanation text as the source, consistent with the pattern from Batches 20-23. This is, proportionally, the most PYQ-book-only-heavy batch in the entire Geography build, matching this sub-topic's inherently miscellaneous/grab-bag nature.

A.3 Doubts carried from the raw extraction

  • Genuine extraction anomaly, not a source-book error: in `geography_pyq_full.txt`, Question 40's printed answer-explanation text ("Where did the Gaddi tribe's shepherds live in the fourteenth century?") is followed by an explanation paragraph that actually describes the unrelated PMAY-U 2.0 scheme, not the Gaddi tribe — almost certainly a column-alignment/pagination artifact on the PDF's final page rather than a real print error in the underlying exam. §6 states the standard, independently-known answer (Western Himalayas) but flags it as not confirmed against this source's own (mismatched) explanation text.
  • Carrying forward the India-Nepal border doubts already flagged in Batch 1 (Durand Line India-vs-Pakistan/Afghanistan tension; the Kalapani/Lipulekh source dispute) — this batch's Kalapani/Susta Assertion-Reason question (§4) adds the Susta/Narayani-river-course detail on top of those pre-existing doubts, still unresolved in either source textbook.

A.4 Completeness check

This batch completes the 24th and final Geography PYQ sub-topic. Sources drawn on: `human_geography_raw_extraction.md` (Parmar pp.84-86 + Lucent pp.153-154) for population/settlement content — its Census-2011/state-population figures and the Demographic Transition Model were NOT pulled in here since no PYQ in this bucket tests those specific numbers (they remain available for a future Static GK cross-reference, per that file's own A.2 note on Census overlap); `world_map_raw_extraction.md` for the Geographical Timescale, Outwash Plain/Watershed glossary terms, Suez Canal and Chabahar Port facts — the bulk of this very large file (country/capital tables, straits, canals, waterfalls, lakes, deserts, islands, geographical epithets, tribes, the full A-Z geographical-terms glossary) was NOT pulled in, since none of it is tested by this bucket's 40 PYQs; it remains available as a reference file if a future revision pass wants a dedicated World Geography addendum, though no PYQ bucket currently calls for one. The Environmental Movements table (book pp.199-200) is now fully represented across Batches 21 (Narmada Bachao Andolan), 22 (cross-reference), and this batch (Bishnoi, Chipko) — 4 of its 12 movements now have PYQ-tested citations; the remaining 8 (Save Silent Valley, Jungle Bachao Andolan, Navadanya, Tarun Bharat Sangh, Saving the Western Ghats, Beej Bachao Andolan, Tehri Dam Conflict, Maiti Movement) have no located PYQ and are left untested by design. Two long-standing flagged gaps from earlier batches remain genuinely unbuilt and are now formally closed out rather than carried forward again: Parmar's book pp.13-14 Rock-formation/classification content (flagged since Batch 18) has no matching PYQ anywhere in the 24-bucket book and is left unbuilt; Batch 13's "History of Geography as a discipline" content was similarly never matched to a dedicated PYQ. Both are pure textbook content without exam traction and are consciously left out of this keyword-revision series, which prioritises tested material. With this batch, all 24 Geography PYQ sub-topics are built, factchecked and clipchecked — the Geography revision series is complete. factcheck.py/clipcheck.py both clean, 0 clipping problems.
